#5125. Tom Lee’s Bitmine Adds $20M in Ethereum, Seizing on Vitalik’s 'ETH Is Money' Declaration

Tom Lee’s Bitmine Adds $20M in Ethereum, Seizing on Vitalik’s 'ETH Is Money' Declaration

Bitmine Immersion Technologies extended its weekly Ethereum buying streak, adding 9,587 ETH worth roughly $20 million, as chairman Tom Lee pointed to a recent declaration by Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin as validation of the firm’s accumulation strategy.

The NYSE-listed company said Monday its holdings reached 6,070,092 ETH, worth roughly $13.6 billion at a reference price of $2,234 per coin.

That’s 4.97% of Ethereum’s 122.2 million token supply, leaving Bitmine, by its own count, 99% of the way to its goal of owning 5% of the network, a target it calls the “Alchemy of 5%,” reached in just 15 months.

The firm has bought Ethereum every week since launching its treasury strategy on June 30, 2025.

Lee leaned heavily on comments Buterin made at the Devcon conference in Mumbai, where the Ethereum founder said, “I’ve finally made up my mind, ETH is a store of value, ETH is money.” Lee called the declaration “a seismic shift,” arguing it clarifies how ETH will capture value in what he described as a coming “supercycle of tokenization and AI.” (Disclosure: Tom Lee is one of several investors in Dastan, Decrypt’s parent company.)

He contended ETH functions as “pristine and trusted collateral” rather than a cash-flow token, reflecting its position as the layer-1 network favored by financial institutions moving assets onchain.

Buterin elaborated in an X post, laying out why he now sees ETH primarily as a store of value, including his view that fee-burning is weaker than it once seemed and that optimizing for it can conflict with decentralization. Lee framed that reasoning as consistent with Ethereum’s roadmap and its role in the tokenization wave.

Counting cash, other tokens and “moonshot” investments, Bitmine’s total holdings stood at $14.3 billion as of Nov. 8, including 216 Bitcoin, $475 million in cash and marketable securities, a $180 million stake in Beast Industries and a $75 million position in Eightco Holdings, which it bills as a rare public proxy for indirect OpenAI exposure.

Bitmine remains the world’s largest Ethereum treasury and the second-largest crypto treasury overall, trailing only Michael Saylor’s Strategy, which the release said holds about 849,327 BTC. Bitmine has 5,227,037 ETH, roughly 86% of its holdings, staked through its MAVAN platform, with projected annualized staking revenue of about $359 million.

The firm also recently argued the muted market reaction to the record Binance hack showed crypto’s growing resilience.

#5123. Aave passes native Bitcoin lending proposal, but hidden liquidation risks remain

Aave's plan to let users borrow stablecoins against native Bitcoin passed an early governance vote and awaits final approval before launch.

Aave's proposal to let holders borrow stablecoins against native Bitcoin cleared its preliminary ARFC governance vote on Nov. 9, according to Babylon Labs.

The borrowing market still needs final approval before holders can use it on mainnet. Babylon reported 100% support for the plan to connect its Trustless Bitcoin Vaults to Aave V4, and the next step is an Aave Improvement Proposal (AIP) for final on-chain approval.

Babylon reports ARFC passage on Nov. 9. Final AIP approval and a restricted mainnet rollout remain ahead.

BTC stays locked on Bitcoin; Aave recognizes collateral on Ethereum and lends supported stablecoins.

The permissionless route uses WBTC now and native BTC redemption later. This remains a proposed design with liquidation risk.

Native BTC would back stablecoin loans, while WBTC still has a separate liquidation-settlement role.

Vote result attributed to Babylon. Late-2026 target remains conditional. Credit limits are proposed capacity. Diagrams omit operator and governance detail; liquidation risk and WBTC dependencies remain.

Reported governance stages are arranged in sequence, without duration or probability scaling. Selectable diagrams explain two different proposed mechanisms; no forecast or live-liquidity calculation is shown.

Vote result attributed to Babylon. Late-2026 target remains conditional. Credit limits are proposed capacity. Diagrams omit operator and governance detail; liquidation risk and WBTC dependencies remain.

Under the proposed change, BTC would remain locked on Bitcoin, while an Ethereum-based accounting record would let Aave recognize it. Borrowers would not need to supply a freely transferable wrapped Bitcoin token.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The governance proposal sets out initial stablecoin credit lines from Aave's Core Hub liquidity pool of $8 million each for USDT and USDC, $1 million for GHO, and $2 million each for USDG and RLUSD.

A separate liquidity pool, the Babylon Hub, would let liquidity providers choose exposure to native Bitcoin collateral, with its own limits.

Risk adviser LlamaRisk recommends a 78% collateral factor for native BTC. The collateral record itself would remain non-borrowable, representing BTC securing a position.

Babylon targets mainnet for late 2026, subject to change and confirmation. It says deployment will start with restrictions on participants and volume, with a schedule and staged rollout plan still to follow. Its current FAQ directs users to a public testnet and gives a fourth-quarter mainnet target.

Keeping the underlying BTC on Bitcoin does not remove liquidation or settlement risk. The Ethereum collateral record would be a transfer-restricted ERC-20 accounting unit, and a borrower could still lose vault collateral through liquidation.

The design also retains a role for Wrapped Bitcoin (WBTC). Under the permissionless liquidation route, BTCVaultSwap would borrow WBTC from an Aave Hub to settle immediately with liquidators.

Permissioned arbitrageurs would later repay that debt and redeem the underlying BTC over several days, as LlamaRisk's assessment describes.

The proposed Core Hub draw cap for that settlement venue is 100 WBTC, separate from the stablecoin lending lines. Permissioned actors can also liquidate and redeem vaults directly without drawing Hub liquidity, although fees or borrower compensation can still require WBTC.

Liquidation seizes whole vaults, even when clearing the debt requires less than a vault's full BTC value, and the borrower receives discounted compensation for that remainder in wrapped BTC.

Prospective borrowers still need final AIP approval and a confirmed rollout schedule before this market moves from governance backing to mainnet access.

#5122. Kimber Digital Securities gains broker-dealer status for tokenized assets

The Plume affiliate plans to offer tokenized US stocks, ETFs and bonds through self-custodial wallets, with traditional shareholder rights preserved.

Kimber Digital Securities, an affiliate of blockchain network Plume, said Tuesday it had registered as a broker-dealer with the US Securities and Exchange Commission and joined the Financial Industry Regulatory Authority.

In its announcement, the company said it plans to offer tokenized US stocks, exchange-traded funds (ETFs) and bonds that investors could hold in self-custodial wallets. The company gave no launch date.

Tokenized stocks are digital tokens that represent company shares or rights tied to those shares. Transfers of the tokens are recorded on a blockchain. The rights investors receive depend on how the tokens are structured.

Unlike wrapped securities, the proposed tokens would represent existing ownership rights recorded through the Depository Trust Company (DTC). The company said investors would retain their dividend and voting rights and could transfer eligible holdings between traditional brokerage accounts and onchain wallets.

The company also plans to offer yield-bearing investment products to stablecoin users, including tokenized shares in funds managed by major US asset managers.

Kimber’s registration comes as other companies prepare to launch around-the-clock trading of tokenized US securities. On Monday, Formation Securities announced plans for a Solana-based trading venue offering tokenized shares of Galaxy Digital, Exodus Movement and Forward Industries, with a launch targeted for early 2027.

Major US exchange operators are pursuing similar initiatives ahead of 2027. The New York Stock Exchange is developing a 24/7 tokenized securities platform using Avalanche for settlement, while Nasdaq is targeting the second quarter of 2027 for its tokenized equity initiative with Kraken’s parent, Payward.

The value of tokenized stocks onchain has surged more than sixfold over the past year, rising to $3.87 billion from $643.3 million in November 2025.

#5118. Urgent Ethereum node patch issued due to hidden security flaws

Ethereum's Lighthouse urged all beacon node operators to install a high-priority security patch ahead of a planned vulnerability disclosure.

Ethereum consensus client Lighthouse released a security patch on Nov. 9 and urged operators of all its beacon nodes to upgrade as soon as possible.

The v8.3.3 release makes that work a high priority for users who stake and those who do not, but the release notice rates validator-client upgrades as low priority for staking users.

As a consensus client, Lighthouse connects with other clients in Ethereum's proof-of-stake network. Its beacon node works with an execution engine to validate transactions in blocks, while the validator client connects to the beacon node and manages keys, according to the Lighthouse Book.

Operators can also run a beacon node without a validator client. Lighthouse's documentation includes data analysis and applications among these non-staking uses, and those operators receive the same high-priority beacon-node instruction as staking users in the v8.3.3 update table.

The project's priority definitions put high-priority work on a timescale of hours or days, to be completed as soon as possible. Low-priority work belongs in the next routine update cycle, with two weeks given as an example.

The beacon-node upgrade is high priority with or without staking. Staking users have a separate, lower-priority validator-client update.

Upgrade the beacon node promptly. The validator-client update has low priority and belongs in the routine cycle.

Beacon nodes used for applications or data analysis have the same high-priority instruction. No validator-client priority applies to this group.

Staking and non-staking beacon nodes are high priority; staking validator clients can use the routine update cycle.

Vulnerability mechanics and potential impact remain undisclosed. High priority describes upgrade urgency. Hours or days and two weeks are illustrative category guidance, not fixed deadlines. No advisory publication date is given.

Map the release's current priority table to operator roles. Hours or days and the routine-cycle example come from Lighthouse's general definitions. Connectors show editorial sequence or component priorities, not network traffic; positions are not a time scale.

Vulnerability mechanics and potential impact remain undisclosed. High priority describes upgrade urgency. Hours or days and two weeks are illustrative category guidance, not fixed deadlines. No advisory publication date is given.

Maintainers say v8.3.3 introduces no breaking changes, but still direct users upgrading from v8.2.x or earlier to read the v8.3.0 release notes, an additional consideration for operators who have skipped intervening versions.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The release says technical details of the vulnerabilities will be disclosed later. Links to security advisories are due to be added once network operators have had time to upgrade.

That approach follows Sigma Prime's Nov. 5 explanation of its disclosure process. The Lighthouse developer says it initially holds back vulnerability information to give users time to update their nodes, then adds details to release notes and GitHub Security Advisories.

The v8.3.3 notice does not report active exploitation or identify losses, and attack mechanics and potential impact remain undisclosed. The high-priority label does not disclose the technical severity or consequences of an attack.

The request is specific to Lighthouse's beacon-node software. For operators running both Lighthouse components, completing a validator-client update alone would still leave the beacon-node instruction outstanding.

Checking which component is being upgraded is key because the two entries in the release table have different schedules. The withheld details also leave claims of theft or an imminent chain halt unsupported by this notice.

#5116. Vitalik Buterin Moves On From ‘Ultrasound Money,’ Says ETH’s Value Is Stability

Vitalik Buterin Moves On From ‘Ultrasound Money,’ Says ETH’s Value Is Stability

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin wants people to stop valuing ETH as a fee-burning token and start treating it as a store of value, arguing that the burn was "never" as strong as it seemed. Binji Pande, founding member of Ethlabs, told Decrypt that he agrees, claiming that it is a more "future-proof store of value" than Bitcoin.

It's a notable shift in messaging from the Ethereum community, after ETH was branded as "ultrasound money" when the network started burning a portion of every transaction fee in 2021. The argument was that as more people used Ethereum, fewer coins would exist in circulation, and fewer coins meant a higher price. Now, however, Buterin claims that isn't as important as people may think.

"We should not feel sad if fee burn is < 10% of breakeven indefinitely; the value of ETH comes from elsewhere," Buterin tweeted on Sunday. That value, he argued, is stability, which comes from Ethereum being decentralized, resistant to censorship, and having strong security.

Expanding on why I believe "ETH as store of value (SoV)" makes much more sense to focus on than "ETH as fee-burning token".

1. ETH as fee-burning token never was as strong as it seemed2. Optimizing for ETH as fee-burning token would demand Ethereum and…

— vitalik.eth (@VitalikButerin) November 8, 2026

Those in favour of the fee-burning structure argued that fees gave ETH a price floor, much like a company's stock may be backed by revenue and profits. From 2021 to 2023, Ethereum burned millions of ETH every year, but Buterin said this was "never a true floor." Most of these burns, he said, came from internal crypto uses like lending and silly NFT purchases.

Connor Howe, co-founder and CEO of infrastructure provider Enso, told Decrypt that Ethereum's layer-2 expansion made the fee-burning argument worse. Connor said that a significant portion of activity has moved onto layer-2s, and users pay "almost nothing" to settle transactions. To optimize a fee-burning token, he added, you'd also want to optimise for higher gas prices—which no one wants.

"If we decide we actually want to optimize ETH for burning fees to justify a present-day ~$270B market cap, then Ethereum would essentially be functioning as a world-scale major corporation," Buterin said. In doing that, Ethereum would lose one of its major selling points: decentralization.

"I agree with Vitalik, and I also believe that ETH is best positioned as a future-proof store of value," Ethlabs' Pande told Decrypt. He highlighted that the network is censorship-resistant, that it has "great distribution," is widely available, and that it is "at the center of future growing sectors such as tokenization, Stablecoins, and DeFi."

A store of value is an asset that holds its worth over time, meaning investors can put their money into it without fearing it will disappear. Historically, gold has been seen as the ultimate store of value, and Bitcoin supporters have pitched it as a "digital gold."

Pande, however, argues that both fall short compared to Ethereum.

"Gold has held the SoV role for a long time, but given the world we live in now is much more globalized, we need increased portability, something gold simply cannot offer," Pande told Decrypt. "Storing gold is simply not useful either past a certain point; you reach physical limits."

When it comes to Bitcoin as a store of value, Pande expressed concern that it isn't "future-proof" as AI and quantum threats loom.

Ethereum has been preparing for those threats for a while. In January, the Ethereum Foundation formed a dedicated post-quantum team and, more recently, researchers have debated the potential AI-powered threats. By contrast, the Bitcoin community appears more fractured on how to tackle the quantum threat.

"Security is ingrained more deeply into Ethereum’s culture than anywhere else," Julian Ma, co-founder of Ethlabs, told Decrypt. "AI being used for cyberattacks and potentially breaking cryptography strengthens ETH's position as a programmable store-of-value asset because the years of preparation and large investments into security and formal verification of the Ethereum protocol and its applications are about to pay off."

Pande added that newer assets can’t compete with ETH as a store of value, because there’s no way to verify they’re fairly distributed. The Ethereum Foundation owns less than 0.2% of ETH, he said, and the token has traded for 11 years.

“Trying to distribute or mine a new asset as a SoV in the lord’s 2026 is like trying to hide a sack of meat in a sea of piranhas,” he said. “It can and WILL get centralized.”

Not everyone thinks Ethereum’s leadership got here quickly enough, though. Howe said the Ethereum Foundation “needs to start to actually listen and look at the applications building on top of their network.”

“I think he is right,” Howe said of Buterin's recent tweet, “and I think it took too long to say it.”

#5113. Coinbase wins national trust charter, and strict OCC mandates apply

Coinbase's federal trust charter is effective, authorizing OCC-supervised crypto custody and a gradual transfer of client accounts.

Coinbase National Trust Company’s federal custody charter is now effective, moving the exchange’s institutional custody expansion beyond preliminary approval.

The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency’s (OCC) application register records Nov. 6 as the effective date. A final conditional approval letter dated Nov. 6 says the bank met its preopening requirements and authorizes it to open on Nov. 9.

That gives the new entity permission to provide cryptocurrency custody under federal fiduciary supervision. The OCC has also approved an arrangement to transfer accounts from Coinbase’s New York trust company over time.

Coinbase announced final approval on Nov. 9, saying the charter would bring a consistent federal framework to its custody business. The milestone follows the April 2 preliminary decision.

The OCC’s final approval permits cryptocurrency custody in a fiduciary capacity. That subjects the national trust company’s custody services to fiduciary duties and standards of behavior.

The letter also discloses a Sept. 1 agreement between Coinbase Custody Trust Company and the new national bank providing for a gradual transition of custody-service rights and responsibilities from the New York trust company to the national trust company.

The OCC approved the acquisition and assumption of those accounts and assets under an exemption from certain restrictions on transactions between banks and affiliates.

The charter permits fiduciary custody. A separate agreement provides for a gradual account transfer.

OCC authorization brings fiduciary custody into the national trust company’s permitted scope.

The September 1 agreement provides for a gradual transfer from the New York trust company to the national trust company.

Individual account completion remains undisclosed.

The dates mark legal milestones, not a verified operating launch. The three-year migration horizon is the April plan.

Qualitative process diagram from the OCC register, April and final November letters, and Coinbase announcement. Dates label distinct legal milestones; timeline spacing is not proportional. Dashed account-transfer connector denotes a disclosed plan rather than a completed movement.

The dates mark legal milestones, not a verified operating launch. The three-year migration horizon is the April plan.

That gives the planned transition regulatory authorization. The final letter describes an intention to begin migration, while Coinbase’s Nov. 9 announcement supplies no completed-transfer figures or client-by-client schedule.

The April plan envisioned moving the entire custody business over the national bank’s three-year initial operating period.

The November conditions supersede those imposed in April, and they keep the bank within trust-company operations and related activities in its business plan.

During its first three operating years, significant deviations require 60 days’ prior notice and written OCC non-objection. The final letter specifically includes material changes to products and services and any offering of transactional services for custody-account customers.

The bank must also maintain at least $60 million in tier 1 capital, prescribed liquid-asset buffers and a separate reserve covering 180 days of operating expenses during that period.

The April decision described an uninsured institution, and the final approval preserves the limited trust-company scope.

#5111. Ava Labs exec says NYSE deal marks end of blockchain’s ‘general purpose era’

Ava Labs’ John Nahas said the collaboration with the NYSE shows institutions moving past pilots toward purpose-built onchain market infrastructure.

Traditional finance is moving past one-size-fits-all blockchains, according to John Nahas, chief business officer at Ava Labs, the company behind the Avalanche network.

Nahas told Cointelegraph that the planned NYSE-affiliated tokenized securities platform, which will use Avalanche technology for clearing and settlement, illustrates that financial institutions want more control over how their blockchains operate.

“Capital markets need reliable performance and predictable cost settlement, certainty and control over the network and its participants,” he said. “It’s really important for this chain to be purpose built for the needs of NYSE.”

Nahas argued that the crypto industry’s preference for general-purpose blockchains and its reluctance to adapt to the compliance rules financial institutions face have slowed adoption.

“Crypto always wants to put the crypto part front and center,” Nahas said. He sees a shift toward blockchains designed for specific use cases. “I look at this as the end of the general purpose era,” he said.

Related: Avalanche’s AVAX leads crypto gains following NYSE tokenization deal

The NYSE move, announced on Monday, comes as more companies object to third parties tokenizing their shares for trading on OKXICE, a separate venue proposed by crypto exchange OKX and NYSE parent Intercontinental Exchange (ICE). OKXICE listed 67 companies that had objected as of Nov. 5, including Apple, Amazon, Meta and Tesla.

Nahas put the pushback down partly to a lack of understanding among incumbents, who he said see crypto as a speculative “wild west.” He said a “wholesale change” to market infrastructure built around institutions’ needs would make the potential efficiency gains clearer and expected objecting companies would “come around.”

Dennis Lee, the NYSE’s head of digital issuance, said the exchange will be “picking the right technology for each job,” as its develops the platform.

Although the NYSE-affiliated platform will support equities issued on a variety of blockchains, the settlement ledger had to allow the exchange to “control who can take part and keep costs predictable,” both elements Lee said Avalanche met. On public blockchains, such as Ethereum, institutions can restrict access to their own applications, while the underlying networks remain open. Congestion can also drive up transaction fees.

The NYSE spent a year testing Avalanche, giving Ava Labs time to tailor its approach to the exchange’s needs, Nahas said.

“The problem is general purpose chains don’t want to or most of the time cannot adapt to the existing frameworks,” he said.

Nahas said the ability to adapt matters most when regulations are still taking shape. “Nobody wants to be on the wrong side of a line that didn’t exist the day before,” he said. In his view, building around the rules financial institutions already follow can help projects move forward while regulators clarify how those rules apply to crypto.

Asked what the “blue wave” in the House and Senate could mean for the US crypto industry, Nahas said he remained optimistic. “The landscape has changed, but the underlying fact is that this train has left the station,” he said. “You see that today in a big way.”

#5110. Solana Set for Another Tokenized Stock Venue as Formation Notifies the SEC

Solana Set for Another Tokenized Stock Venue as Formation Notifies the SEC

A new firm is lining up to become one of the first to trade tokenized U.S. stocks onchain, notifying the Securities and Exchange Commission that it intends to run a tokenized securities venue on Solana.

Formation Securities said Monday it had filed the notice to operate under the SEC’s “Innovation Exemption,” the conditional relief the agency issued in September that lets eligible tokenized stocks trade through permissioned automated market maker pools on qualifying venues.

Trading is expected to begin in early 2027, with tokenized shares of Exodus Movement, Forward Industries and Galaxy Digital anticipated at launch.

The pitch is to deliver the benefits of blockchain trading, 24/7 markets and near-instant settlement, without stripping away the rights investors expect from real stock.

Under the exemption, eligible tokens must carry the same voting and dividend rights as traditional shares, and each trade settles atomically onchain, a feature meant to cut settlement risk. A company’s transfer agent still keeps the official shareholder record, while every trade posts to the public ledger in real time.

“Tokenized stocks will succeed only if public companies see the value in them, and we intend to build this market together with issuers,” said Christopher Montagano, president of Formation Securities, which licenses its trading technology from Solana-based exchange Orca.

Tokenization firms Securitize and Superstate are expected to supply tokenized shares, with Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo noting its tokens are each backed by a real share held by its broker-dealer.

Access will be limited to verified investors, who can reach the venue through Formation’s platform and distributors including Alpaca, CoinList’s Passage, custodians Anchorage, BitGo and Fireblocks, and wallet providers Solflare and Squads.

The move adds another entrant to a fast-crowding race to put stocks onchain. The NYSE recently tapped Solana rival Avalanche to settle its own planned tokenized securities platform, Securitize launched tokenized stocks on Solana, and an OKX-ICE venue is also in the works, though every Magnificent Seven stock opted out of that one.

The effort builds on Project Open, a proposal a coalition including Orca, Superstate and the Solana Policy Institute submitted to the SEC in 2025.

Formation chose Solana, it said, for its speed, low costs and track record with tokenized assets, noting the network hosts more than $5 billion in tokenized real-world assets.

#5107. 96% of older Bitcoin being sent to exchanges is now underwater

About 5,950 BTC reached exchanges below estimated acquisition cost on Nov. 10, accounting for 96% of that day's long-term-holder deposits.

Bitcoin's long-term holding cohort sent about 5,950 BTC to exchanges below estimated acquisition cost on Nov. 10. Underwater coins dominated this cohort's deposits, even as the volume arriving below cost fell from the previous day.

The loss-inflow series from analytics provider CoinGlass showed a 29.22% daily decline. Its total cohort inflows reached 6,182.67 BTC, including 232.72 BTC arriving above cost. Below-cost coins therefore accounted for about 96% of older-holder exchange deposits that day.

November 10, 2026: below-cost deposits fell 29.22% in a day but made up about 96% of the long-term cohort's exchange inflows.

Loss-making deposits dominated this cohort's exchange arrivals despite the daily decline.

Gross inflows for one cohort, not all Bitcoin or net reserves. Exchange labels and estimated costs affect classification. Deposits do not establish sales.

Daily UTC CoinGlass inputs: loss 5,949.9482 BTC, profit 232.721 BTC, total 6,182.6693 BTC. Loss/total ×100 = 96.235912%, rounded to about 96%; bar uses the unrounded ratio. Displayed BTC rounds to two decimals. The 29.22% decline is provider-reported. Purpose branches encode no quantities.

Gross inflows for one cohort, not all Bitcoin or net reserves. Exchange labels and estimated costs affect classification. Deposits do not establish sales.

The deposits represent potential trading supply under loss pressure, but transfers to exchanges do not establish completed sales.

CoinGlass defines the long-term cohort around a 155-day holding boundary. Its loss-inflow measure counts BTC sent to identified exchange addresses below estimated acquisition cost.

A separate profitability measure gives an earlier view of older-coin activity. CoinGlass reports a long-term-holder spent output profit ratio, or LTH-SOPR, of 0.82063 for Nov. 9, below the break-even level of 1.

The ratio compares the aggregate dollar value of older coins when spent with their value at creation. A reading below 1 means those spent coins moved at an aggregate loss. Unlike the deposit figures, this measure covers spending across the network.

Separate trading data also pointed to seller aggression. CryptoSlate reported on Nov. 10 that Glassnode's Nov. 9 snapshot showed higher spot turnover alongside negative spot cumulative volume delta. Negative spot cumulative volume delta means sell-initiated volume exceeded buy-initiated volume.

The next useful signals are whether below-cost deposit volume continues to fall and whether its share of cohort inflows declines. Nov. 10's smaller flow still carried a predominantly underwater mix, keeping potential supply pressure in focus.

#5105. 5 Best Acupressure Pens for Targeted Massage, According to Experts

Here’s how to use one, and what to expert.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

There's a lot coming at you on a daily basis to stress you out. But even though you probably lean into the big de-stressors like exercising regularly, doing breathing exercises on demand, and talk therapy as needed, sometimes you just need a way to dial down the tension building up inside you without visiting a provider's office or hitting the gym. That's where acupressure pens come in.

These handheld tools, which are sometimes called acupuncture pens, are designed to target pressure points in your body to help relieve tension and pain. "Acupressure stimulates acupuncture points without using needles," says Tom Ingegno, DACM, acupuncturist and founder of Charm City Integrative Health. "It lets people take a more active role in their own health without necessarily seeing a licensed [acupuncture] practitioner."

An acupressure pen has a shape that's optimized to stimulate acupuncture points, or areas on the body that are linked to certain health benefits, explains Margie Navarro, DAOM, doctor of acupuncture at Vivacity Healthcare in New York City. "The tool has a rather small head that is smooth and rounded, so that it is a relatively comfortable way to stimulate pressure on a small area or point," she says.

People use these tools to ease anxiety, headaches, and general pain, and there is some data to suggest acupressure in general may be helpful in treating all of these. (Because each acupressure pen is slightly different, it's a little tricky to prove that they'll work for these issues.)

Still, acupuncture experts say these pens are unlikely to hurt if you want to give them a try. "It gives people a way to play an active role in their health," Navarro says. "Even just a sense of agency can improve health outcomes." Sounds like something you can get behind? These acupressure pens can get you started.

There are a ton of acupressure pens out there to choose from, and most can be used all over your body. Odacite's Mon Ami tool was specifically designed with facial acupressure in mind, with a rounded tip on one end and a spatula-scoop hybrid on the other. That allows users to apply products like moisturizers and serums to targeted areas, before massaging it in.

This metal pen feels cool to the touch, creating a soothing feel as you work out muscle tension. It also comes with a pretty pink carry bag to make it easy to tote it with you while you travel. Just be aware that you'll need to clean this regularly, especially if you use it with beauty products.

Most acupressure pens are inexpensive, but the Wellgler pen is a mere $7, making it the cheapest option on our list. The acupressure pen has two different rounded heads, allowing you to really get in there for smaller areas you want to target, like TMJ muscles. But the larger end is also a good option for massaging the rest of your face, and even your sinuses.

This pen feels a little weightier than you'd expect for $7—that can be a pro or a con, depending on your personal preference in an acupressure pen. It also has contoured grips on both ends, so you can get a comfortable, secure grasp while massaging or pressing on any given area.

The big draw of an acupressure pen is its tips—they're what you use to target pressure points, after all. But the Feelfree acupressure pen has a long, tapered tip that you can use to roll over various parts of your body, giving you even more options than "just" acupressure.

The dual rounded ends feature different sizes, so you can target your TMJ muscles with one end, before easing tension in your neck with the other, larger end. It comes with a microfiber cloth for easy cleaning, along with a travel bag, so you can easily do acupressure on the go.

Most acupressure pens you'll find are made of some kind of metal, both for a cooling feel against your skin and for ease of cleaning. The Yutiann acupressure pen is made of wood for a gentler feel. This acupressure pen is relatively large, at 6.1 centimeters, giving you more options for use.

You can use the pen and its length on your hands and face, along with your back and feet. Multiple grooves near the top of the pen create a molded feel for a comfortable grip that you may not get as easily with smaller metal pens. Just be aware that wooden tools aren't as easy to clean as their metal counterparts. This pen's larger size also means the smaller tip may not be petite enough to target teeny tense muscles.

This Rena Chris set gives you a little of everything for working out stress and pain in your face. It features a contoured acupressure pen, along with a gua sha tool, both made of a sturdy stainless steel. So, you can do targeted acupressure, followed by gua sha to ease tension and shuttle away swelling.

This set has a reflexology guide to show various pressure points on your hands, feet, and ears that you may want to touch to soothe whatever ails you. It also comes with a cleaning cloth to make clean-up a breeze. Just be aware that there's no included travel bag, making you on your own for that one.

You have a few options for use with an acupressure pen. In general, these are designed to stimulate pressure points in your body. Most people use acupressure pens for some type of pain relief, whether it's headaches or TMJ discomfort. But these pens can also be used to ease anxiety and stress.

Many acupressure pens are made of medical-grade stainless steel. However, some are made from brass or other metals. You can also find acupressure pens made of wood, although these are less common.

Every pen is slightly different, making it important to read the manufacturer's directions first. But you can typically clean an acupressure pen by wiping it with a soft cloth before you use it.

Acupressure pens shouldn't break your budget, and most cost $25 or less. "The key is the stimulation of the point, not necessarily the tool you're using," Ingegno says. "You don't need an expensive device to practice acupressure, but a pen can make it easier."

There is data to suggest that acupressure can help with easing a range of pain and discomfort. "Manual pressure can have therapeutic benefits in a variety of conditions, including muscular pain," says Navarro. But you need to know which areas to target. And, of course, not everyone will feel pain or stress relief from acupressure, regardless of whether you get it from a pen or a provider.

Ingegno suggests learning about pressure points in the body before using an acupressure pen. "I used to recommend books, but these days there's so much information online that it's pretty easy to find a starting point," he says. Once you've figured out the area you want to target, he suggests applying gentle to moderate pressure for about 30 to 60 seconds. "Some points may feel a little sore or tender when pressed, and that's not necessarily a bad thing, but you shouldn't be causing yourself pain," Ingegno says. "More pressure isn't always better."

You can apply steady pressure on a point or gently rub or massage the area with the tool, according to Ingegno. "Neither approach is wrong, and both can be useful," he says.

There are electronic acupressure pens, which deliver small electrical impulses to pressure points. "Follow the manufacturer's instructions, since there are several different types available and they don't all work the same way," Ingegno says.

Most people can safely use acupressure pens, according to Ingegno. But there are a few caveats. If you're pregnant, he recommends taking a pass. If you're using blood thinners, Ingegno also suggests being gentle, since you may bruise more easily. "Electronic acupressure pens also require a few additional precautions," Ingegno says. "People with pacemakers or other implanted electronic medical devices should avoid electrical stimulation unless their healthcare provider has specifically cleared them."

If you have broken skin or areas where the tissue is already damaged, Navarro recommends avoiding using the acupressure pen on that area.

She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#5104. Nvidia's $350 Billion Scare: What Happens If Jensen Huang Leaves?

Nvidia's $350 Billion Scare: What Happens If Jensen Huang Leaves?

Nvidia shares plunged as much as 8% Monday, briefly threatening to erase roughly $350 billion in market value as investors weighed the possibility that CEO Jensen Huang could lose his job over an alleged Chinese intelligence operation.

For a company that has become the face of the artificial intelligence boom, that's a rather expensive scandal. And the investigation is just getting started.

Nvidia's board opened an independent investigation following reports that Huang had a romantic relationship with a business executive whom U.S. intelligence believes was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security.

Huang acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a message to employees, apologized and pledged to cooperate. Nvidia's board said it had no reason to believe company secrets or U.S. national security had been compromised, and expressed confidence in his leadership.

The allegations have not established that Huang disclosed sensitive information or knowingly cooperated with foreign intelligence. Investors, however, are already confronting the possibility of a much bigger problem.

Nvidia's market capitalization had declined from roughly $5.78 trillion on October 6 to $4.51 trillion by November 6, according to YCharts. That's approximately $1.27 trillion in lost value before the board publicly announced its investigation.

The latest controversy adds another risk to a stock already under pressure from questions about the sustainability of massive AI infrastructure spending.

Nvidia also carries enough weight to move the broader market. A November 6 snapshot placed the company at 6.93% of the SPDR S&P 500 ETF, which tracks the market-capitalization-weighted index of major U.S. companies.

At that weight, a 5.6% decline in Nvidia alone would subtract approximately 0.39 percentage points from the S&P 500's performance, assuming everything else remained unchanged—which most likely will not happen, considering Nvidia is the main hardware provider for many other companies in the country, including some in the top 10.

Yet the S&P 500 rose 1.48% Monday morning, while the Nasdaq gained 1.87%. Other semiconductor stocks also rallied, suggesting investors were treating Nvidia's troubles as largely company-specific rather than abandoning the broader AI trade.

Huang has led Nvidia since co-founding it in 1993. Replacing him would involve considerably more than finding another executive capable of presenting a new GPU.

The 63-year-old CEO has built an unusually centralized management structure and maintains important relationships with customers, suppliers and governments. Analysts have questioned whether Nvidia has an obvious successor.

Chris Weston, head of research at Pepperstone, told Reuters he didn't expect Huang to resign. But if investors genuinely believed his departure was imminent, the stock's reaction could be an "absolute bloodbath."

Bloodbath or not, the stock would be exposed to a lot of uncertainty. After all, Huang is the man responsible for building a whole imperium. It would be similar to Mark Zuckerberg leaving Meta, Elon Musk leaving Tesla or Vitalik Buterin disowning Ethereum. Beyond pure market analysis, Huang is the name behind Nvidia and market sentiment can react to that.

Dan Ives of Yorkville Ives & Co. similarly described Huang as not replaceable, although he acknowledged the investigation could ultimately result in his removal.

An extramarital affair alone does not automatically justify dismissing a CEO. Unless the scandal becomes a reputational risk for Nvidia, it’s possible that Huang escapes the scandal and markets recover as long as the company continues being extremely profitable. The more consequential question is whether Huang's conduct exposed sensitive information, created security vulnerabilities, or compromised Nvidia's business interests.

So far, the company has announced no findings establishing such failures.

A resignation could trigger another sharp selloff, particularly without a credible succession plan. But there is no reliable way to calculate how far the stock would fall, or whether investors would ultimately welcome new management.

Nvidia has been in a sustained bullish trend ever since ChatGPT entered the market in 2023. That said, its weekly chart was already flashing warning signs before the allegations emerged.

The stock traded near $179.34 Monday, significantly below its 50-week exponential moving average of approximately $199.16. That indicator tracks the longer-term price trend while assigning greater weight to recent trading.

The breakdown is particularly significant because Nvidia had spent much of its recent bull market above that average. Its last comparable breach occurred during the 2025 correction and the pre-ChatGPT era before that in 2022-2023.

The stock had already crossed below that indicator before the scandal, meaning the investigation accelerated an existing bearish trend rather than causing the entire breakdown.

Based on the weekly chart, several levels stand out:

These are potential price targets based on technical analysis, and a recovery above $190, followed by a move back above the 50-week EMA near $199, would improve the technical outlook.

Monday's weekly candle also remains unfinished, so a confirmed weekly close below the indicator would carry more weight than an intraday move.

President Donald Trump has already defended Huang, describing him as an honorable person who has done an exceptional job for the United States.

"I think he probably has an excuse for it. You have to hear it," Trump told reporters Sunday.

The president's support could help Huang politically, but the controversy comes as Nvidia navigates Washington's restrictions on advanced AI chip exports to China.

Huang has repeatedly advocated allowing Nvidia to sell processors to Chinese customers. Allegations involving a suspected intelligence operation could complicate those negotiations, even without evidence that the CEO knowingly participated in espionage.

China's Foreign Ministry has rejected the allegations as baseless.

Congressional Republicans have also requested a private briefing on Nvidia's security procedures, with lawmakers seeking Huang's agreement to a briefing date by November 11, according to Axios.

Investors have another important date approaching. Nvidia is scheduled to report its quarterly earnings on November 17, giving the market an opportunity to assess its underlying business against the uncertainty surrounding its CEO.

The board has not disclosed when its independent investigation will conclude.

#5102. US card debt hits $1.29 trillion as Bitcoin loans expose a collateral divide

US card debt reached $1.29 trillion. Bitcoin-backed loans advertise lower rates, but borrowers must weigh fees and the risk of an automatic collateral sale.

US credit-card balances reached $1.29 trillion in the third quarter, the New York Fed reported on Nov. 10. Bitcoin-backed loans advertise lower borrowing rates, but the comparison comes with a different risk: losing the collateral in an automatic sale.

The New York Fed's report puts card balances $22 billion higher than the previous quarter and $52 billion higher than a year earlier.

Separately, the Federal Reserve's latest measure of interest assessed on card accounts was 22.36%, while Coinbase advertises crypto-backed borrowing from 5.1%.

That gap can translate into substantially lower modeled interest costs, but accessing it requires assets. A $10,000 payout would require $25,500 in Bitcoin at a chosen 40% initial loan-to-value ratio once the processing fee is financed.

The rate may be attractive, but the collateral can be sold automatically if the loan becomes too large relative to its backing.

Consider a constant $10,000 card balance for one year, with no principal repayments and simple interest at the Fed's 22.36% measure, and the modeled interest cost is $2,236.

For Coinbase's variable-rate product, assume a payout worth $10,000 in USDC and hold the advertised 5.1% rate constant for a year. Coinbase's variable-loan processing fee is 2% on the first $250,000, with 1% charged on amounts above that tier.

The $200 fee brings the opening debt to $10,200, and simple interest on that amount is $520.20. Adding the fee produces a modeled first-year borrowing cost of $720.20.

The gross difference is about $1,516. This simplified illustration gives neither a Bitcoin borrower quote nor guaranteed savings, and it does not calculate a full APR. It excludes platform fees, compounding, rate changes, conversion costs, and liquidation effects.

Coinbase says a platform fee may apply monthly to variable-rate loans, and the lending rate also changes with supply and demand, so 5.1% cannot be treated as a promised yearlong cost. The actual offer determines how much of the apparent gap survives.

On the card side, the 22.36% figure in the Federal Reserve's Nov. 6 consumer-credit release appears in the August and third-quarter observations for commercial-bank accounts assessed interest. The corresponding figure for all accounts was 21.19%.

The Fed annualizes finance charges relative to the average daily balances on which those charges were imposed, excluding accounts without finance charges. The rate cannot simply be applied to every dollar of the New York Fed's $1.29 trillion estimate.

Coinbase says eligible, verified US customers outside New York can borrow without a credit check. Borrowing capacity depends on eligible collateral, with limits shown in the product. Removing the credit check replaces it with an asset requirement.

Through the integration, pledged Bitcoin is converted into Coinbase Wrapped Bitcoin (cbBTC) and transferred to a smart contract on Morpho, a lending protocol on Base.

Collateral held there is marked locked and nontradable, although qualifying excess collateral can be withdrawn within loan-to-value (LTV) limits. Variable-rate loans have no scheduled minimum payments or fixed due date, but interest continues accruing. Payment flexibility still requires monitoring the collateral.

LTV divides outstanding debt by the collateral's current market value, so a $10,000 debt at an illustrative 40% initial LTV needs $25,000 of Bitcoin before fees.

Including the financed $200 processing fee raises that requirement to $25,500 to preserve the same starting ratio.

If debt reaches the market's liquidation threshold relative to collateral, a sale can happen automatically. For Bitcoin-backed borrowing, Coinbase describes repayment of debt and interest with an additional 4.38% penalty. Any Bitcoin left after the required liquidation returns to the Coinbase account.

For a hypothetical Bitcoin-backed loan with an 86% liquidation threshold and constant debt, a loan beginning at 40% LTV reaches that threshold after a roughly 53.5% fall in collateral value. A separate loan beginning at 60% LTV reaches the same threshold after about a 30.2% fall.

A larger cushion requires more collateral or less borrowing. Coinbase also identifies protocol security, liquidity, and Coinbase Smart Wallet issues as risks, adding dependencies alongside the obligation to repay.

The New York Fed said credit-card transitions into early and serious delinquency improved slightly in the third quarter. Annualized flows into serious delinquency were 6.9%, versus 7.1% a year earlier.

In a separate Nov. 10 analysis, New York Fed economists said card delinquency increased most sharply after 2022 among borrowers in lower-income neighborhoods, widening the gap with higher-income areas.

For someone with sufficient eligible assets, crypto-backed borrowing may reduce interest costs after fees. For someone without them, the advertised rate does not resolve the financing problem, as borrowing capacity hinges on the assets available to pledge.

The useful comparison is the fee-inclusive offer against the amount of crypto committed and the liquidation buffer left behind. A lower borrowing rate can coexist with a new risk of losing Bitcoin through an automatic sale when the borrower intended to keep it.

#5100. Across Protocol warns users to avoid app amid reports of drained wallets

The protocol is investigating a potential security incident affecting its domain, while several users have reported losing funds after interacting with the platform.

Crosschain bridging protocol Across, which allows users to transfer digital assets between Ethereum and other blockchain networks, warned users Tuesday not to access its app or connect their wallets while it investigates a potential security incident.

In a Tuesday X post, Across advised users against signing transactions or approvals until further notice, without specifying the nature of the incident or whether its website had been compromised.

On X, one user claimed to have lost $9,000 in USDC, while another reported losing 7,053 USDT0 across seven transactions on the Plasma blockchain.

A third user said their wallet was drained after using the bridge several times, alleging that the website had been compromised. Across asked the users for transaction details to investigate their reports.

Cointelegraph has not independently verified the reports. Across Protocol did not immediately respond to Cointelegraph’s request for more details.

Across Protocol responds to user reporting $9,000 USDC loss. Source: X user bilm01

In a separate security incident, NEAR AI disclosed Monday that attackers exploited a vulnerability in its AI infrastructure, exposing account details belonging to approximately 100 users and data from 18 AI agents.

According to a Nov. 9 incident report, attackers exploited a remote code execution vulnerability in SGLang, an open-source AI inference engine, to gain access to an internal server and other infrastructure. The compromised data included user names, email addresses, agent chat histories, settings and logs, while two customers had API keys exposed.

NEAR AI said attackers may also have observed requests processed on the compromised server, including those running in confidential mode. The company said it had patched the vulnerability, revoked exposed credentials and restored access to its cloud services.

#5098. Bitcoin in Limbo as Stocks Rebound Ahead of US Inflation Report

Bitcoin in Limbo as Stocks Rebound Ahead of US Inflation Report

After a tumultuous week across traditional and digital asset markets, Bitcoin has returned to prices last seen in early August, when the cryptocurrency mounted an impressive 25% gain in a single month.

This time, though, $75,000 looks a bit different for crypto investors, now that BTC is on the way back down. So where does Bitcoin go next? Here’s what the charts look like.

U.S. stocks rebounded Monday as oil prices fell, with the S&P 500 up 1.9% and the Nasdaq Composite up 2.2% by 11:30 a.m. Eastern. Brent crude fell 4.3% to about $94 a barrel, giving the broader market a lift even as Nvidia shares slid in a move that spooked tech investors.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics is due to release the October consumer price index tomorrow. The report tracks what households pay for goods and services, and it lands after a stronger-than-expected jobs report pushed traders to price in roughly an 80% chance of a quarter-point Federal Reserve interest rate hike in December.

Bitcoin was up 0.7% at $74,559 after touching its lowest level in more than two months. The rebound followed a wave of forced selling in crypto derivatives, with a CoinGlass snapshot showing $1.2 billion in liquidations over 24 hours.

Exchanges liquidate positions when traders can no longer cover their losses with the collateral they’ve posted. About $1.04 billion of the total came from longs, or bets on higher prices. Bitcoin positions accounted for $324 million, nearly 87% of it in longs.

Bitcoin rallied from roughly $57,700 to $87,350 before sliding back toward $75,000. The coin is currently trading at $74,685 after an early dip to $73,667, with the week’s candle still open.

The 50-week exponential moving average, or EMA, sits near $78,250, above the 200-week EMA at about $69,790. Both averages give more weight to recent prices, with the shorter one responding faster.

Their order still favors the longer-term uptrend, though Bitcoin is trading below the faster average, and the gap has narrowed considerably, which increases the possibility of a death cross in the future.

The Relative Strength Index, or RSI, compares recent gains with recent losses on a scale of zero to 100. At 47.8, it’s close to the midpoint, with a slight tilt toward sellers and some distance from 30, the level traders typically call oversold.

The Average Directional Index, or ADX, measures how strongly prices are trending in either direction. The chart’s reading of 21.9 is below the commonly used 25 threshold for a strong trend, even though its accompanying directional indicators give buyers a slight edge.

On the way down, $74,400 is the first area where buyers could step in. Bitcoin has already traded through it this week, so a weekly close below it would put $72,550 in view, followed by the $69,000 area around the 200-week EMA.

On a rebound, the first area where sellers could stall the move is near $76,040, followed by the 50-week EMA around $78,250. A move through that average would put $80,360 back in play, with the prior rebound high near $87,350 beyond it.

#5097. Tokenized stock trading hits $2.7B even though trades can cost 3% more

Modeled $10,000 trades exposed wide cost differences between stock tokens, even when their quoted prices stayed close to the underlying shares.

Tokenized stocks recorded about $3 billion in decentralized-exchange transactions over seven days, according to figures reported on Nov. 4.

US stocks rallied that day as Republicans lost unified control of Washington, with the S&P 500 up 1.6%, the Dow up about 3% and the Nasdaq up 1.4%.

A modeled $10,000 round trip in tokenized Coinbase shares carried a quote gap near $303 that week, about 3% of the trade.

Fillable's Nov. 3 to Nov. 9 study of Solana tokenized stocks quoted buys and sells every 15 minutes during regular US hours, using recent IEX trades as the reference price.

It accounted for pool fees and price slippage, and the figures model quote gaps from simulated trades. An S&P 500 ETF token (SPYx) carried a gap of $20.60, or 0.21% of a $10,000 trade.

Tesla's TSLAx came in at $43.60, Nvidia's NVDAx at $44.30, and Apple's AAPLx at $88.80. Coinbase's COINx reached $303, Robinhood's HOODx $371, and Palantir's PLTRx $699.

Selected Solana stock tokens during regular US hours on Nov. 3-9. Quotes were simulated every 15 minutes against recent IEX trades, pool fees and price impact are included.

Source: Fillable — Solana tokenized stock prices, Nov. 3-9

COINx's gap is about 14.7 times SPYx's, and PLTRx's is about 34 times. COINx carried a median buy premium near 153 basis points and a sale discount near 149.

In an illustration with those medians, $10,000 in USDC buys about $9,849 of exposure at the reference price, and an immediate reversal returns about $9,703.

Fillable measured 22 stocks, and 19 had a midpoint within 0.1% of the underlying share price at a $1,000 trade size.

A midpoint reflects where a token is marked, and a buyer moving through a pool receives a price shaped by available liquidity, the pool's curve, and the trade size. The study excludes network fees, some token-to-token conversions, and the savings from splitting an order across pools, so it covers less than an all-in wallet-to-wallet cost.

Coin Metrics' Nov. 3 State of the Network found tokenized Nvidia order-book depth within 1% of price down 19% on average when US markets were closed on Bybit and MEXC. On Hyperliquid, order-book depth for the same token fell about 33%.

Tokenized Nvidia prices showed weak correlations of 0.24 and 0.30 with the next US open in the evening and overnight windows, and alignment improved as the open approached. Fillable's Nov. 9 index shows a median $17.90 round-trip quote cost across ten major stocks and funds, using each asset's cheapest pool across four chains.

The median was $22.70 during regular US market hours and $15.80 when US markets were closed. Unlike the venue-specific Nvidia order books, this index selects each asset’s cheapest eligible pool, so the two studies do not measure the same markets.

Brokerages already offer extended-hours trading in eligible securities, so the tokenized edge is wallet access and crypto-native settlement, subject to issuer and jurisdictional limits.

Medians for simulated $10,000 round trips across ten fixed stocks and funds, each using its cheapest eligible pool across Solana, Robinhood Chain, Base and BNB Chain on Nov. 9. Fees and price impact are included, network fees excluded.

Source: Fillable 10 — Nov. 9 index and session medians

Bitquery's October study counted $10 billion of tokenized-stock DEX turnover across six issuers and five chains. For Oct. 2–31, 65% of turnover occurred outside regular US hours, and the 100 busiest wallets produced 64% of Coinbase-issued token volume.

About 10% of Robinhood-issued token volume from Oct. 2–31 came from transactions that bought and sold the same token within one transaction, consistent with automated arbitrage, against about 2% for Coinbase-issued tokens.

Adjusting for those round trips cuts Robinhood's monthly lead over Coinbase from $119 million to about $11 million.

NYSE-backed OKXICE faced objections covering 57 of its 63 planned stock tickers. Meanwhile, Dune data showed a median of $92 in holdings per decentralized equity liquidity pool as of Aug. 31.

Of 5,484 pools, 78 held about 81% of measured assets. The $92 is a pool-holdings median, a gauge of capital concentration and separate from execution quality at any venue.

The SEC has said tokenized securities can carry materially different rights depending on structure, and its September Innovation Exemption sets conditions for limited experiments. A token tracking a stock can differ from the stock in ownership, voting, dividend, and redemption rights.

If higher volume attracts market makers and more liquidity becomes available for $10,000 orders, quote gaps could narrow across major tokens, including in off-hours sessions.

The measures to watch are the share of $10,000 orders executable within 50 basis points of the underlying stock, depth within 1% of the reference price in and out of regular hours, and wallet concentration.

If activity proves temporary or remains concentrated in arbitrage, liquidity providers could withdraw capital and costs could rise even while the market stays open around the clock. In a trading halt or redemption disruption, a token could carry a theoretical price while holders face exits at unacceptable prices.

A market whose turnover doubles with spreads and depth unchanged has grown busier, and one whose spreads narrow and depth rises has improved the service investors receive.

Execution quality, the cash recovered on a sale, and the rights attached to the token will decide whether 24/7 access is worth its price.

#5092. Binance Hack Launderers Mint Elon Musk and Animal Meme Coins to Move Stolen Crypto

Binance Hack Launderers Mint Elon Musk and Animal Meme Coins to Move Stolen Crypto

North Korea’s hackers have found a novel use for the internet’s favorite joke tokens: laundering a slice of the record $6.4 billion stolen from Binance.

According to a report from blockchain analytics firm Elliptic, the launderers are creating their own worthless meme coins and using them to shuffle stolen funds through decentralized exchanges in a way that breaks the direct onchain link between the thief’s wallet and the one collecting the proceeds.

The launderers mint a brand-new token, pair it with a real asset like Near or a stablecoin in a liquidity pool, pay stolen funds into the pool through one set of wallets to buy the token, then sell it back through a separate set of wallets to withdraw clean-looking assets.

Because the pool sits in the middle, no stolen funds move directly from the buying wallet to the collecting one, and the receiving wallets look like ordinary traders cashing out a meme coin.

Elliptic, in research credited to co-founder Tom Robinson, said the launderers deployed the tactic on Tron and Near, with a close variant on Solana. The clearest example came on Near, where between Nov. 5 and 7 they created at least six home-made tokens, each paired with Near in its own pool.

The coins carried the usual meme-coin trappings: cartoon logos of a bow-wearing monkey, a flag-draped duck, a chimpanzee and a fox, one even featuring an “Elon 2028 President” image of Elon Musk, who has no connection to the tokens. The names ran from the crude (FUK) to a riff on dog coins (SINU).

Each token cost about 25 Near tokens, roughly $90, to create. All six followed the same arc: launched, traded heavily within hours using stolen NEAR, then abandoned, with the leftover liquidity pulled on Nov. 8.

In total, about 117,000 Near, or roughly $430,000, passed through the six tokens before much of it flowed into stablecoins or a cross-chain bridge.

The sums are modest so far, a reminder that the bulk of the haul is still parked onchain. Elliptic earlier found more than $3 billion of the stolen crypto had yet to be laundered, and that the launderers were spinning up a new wallet roughly every 25 seconds to move funds at machine speed.

Crucially, the meme-coin trick doesn’t actually hide the money. Elliptic stressed the trail remains fully traceable onchain; the maneuver only slows investigators down, buying time in what is essentially a race to move funds before they get flagged.

It’s one cheap, disposable layer among many that North Korea’s launderers stack together. Elliptic first observed the tactic last year while tracing the $1.5 billion Bybit theft, also attributed to the TraderTraitor group behind the Binance breach.

For compliance teams, the firm warned, funds arriving from a liquidity pool shouldn’t be assumed clean, especially where freshly minted tokens and a handful of trading wallets are involved.

#5090. Bitcoin holds $73,000 as odds of a December 50 bps rate hike jump

Hot inflation lifted bets on a bigger December Fed hike, but even a quarter-point increase could pressure Bitcoin if officials signal higher rates for longer.

The odds of a 50-basis-point Federal Reserve hike in December rose from 0% to 13.6% on Nov. 10 after October CPI beat forecasts, as Bitcoin fell to an intraday low below $73,000.

Meanwhile, the odds of a 25-basis-point hike climbed from 84% to 86.4%, according to a Nov. 10 CME FedWatch snapshot reported by Barron's.

Headline CPI rose 0.4% versus a 0.3% forecast, and core rose 0.3% versus 0.2%, meeting the condition Governor Christopher Waller set for considering a 50 bps move.

The market is pricing more tightening, with 25 bps the dominant outcome. The no-hike probability dropped from 16% to 0%, so the 25 bps odds rose alongside the 50 bps odds.

Weighting the three outcomes, the expected December hike moved from 21.0 bps to 28.4 bps, up 7.4 bps. A 50/50 split between the two sizes would imply 37.5 bps, so the market sits closer to its old expectation than to a 50 bps base case.

The odds ratio favoring 50 over 25 would have to rise more than sixfold before 50 bps became the likelier size.

Dec. 9 decision probabilities from the CME FedWatch snapshot quoted in the supplied article and attributed there to Barron's. Each bar totals 100%.

Source: CME FedWatch — historical snapshot quoted in supplied article

October's report was hotter than forecast and cooler than September on the headline, which slowed from 0.6% to 0.4%. Core stayed at 0.3% for a third month, and transportation services rose 0.8%.

At a constant 0.3% a month, core inflation compounds to about 3.7% annualized, a run-rate calculation that stands apart from an official annual figure. The BLS omitted an annual October comparison, since the 2025 government shutdown interrupted the underlying data collection.

Waller's threshold opens consideration of a 50 bps hike, and committee backing is a separate matter. The Fed held at 3.75% to 4% on Oct. 28 by a 10-2 vote, and both dissenters wanted 25 bps.

Seasonally adjusted CPI-U changes from the preceding month, as reported in the Nov. 10 release. Core excludes food and energy. Month labels refer to the measured month. BLS omitted most October year-over-year comparisons because October 2025 survey data were unavailable.

Source: BLS — October 2026 CPI release, Table A, retrieved Nov. 10

Three developments could strengthen the case for 50 bps at the FOMC’s December meeting and shape Bitcoin's outlook.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

October core PCE on Nov. 25 would need to confirm CPI, with monthly readings near 0.3% to 0.4% and broad price strength, against September's 0.3% monthly and 3.1% annual core PCE gain.

The Dec. 4 jobs report would need to show a labor market that can absorb more tightening. October payrolls rose 165,000 against a 12-month average of 48,000, unemployment was 4.1%, and hourly earnings rose 0.4%.

Several voting members would also need to back faster tightening publicly, and oil and sticky services costs would need to persist.

PPI arrives Nov. 13, the minutes of the October meeting Nov. 18, and the Fed's communications blackout begins Nov. 28, before the Dec. 4 jobs report.

The decision comes Dec. 9, and November CPI lands Dec. 10, one day after the Fed's decision, so officials decide before the next CPI print arrives. Softer PCE or hiring could weaken the case for a half-point increase.

A Reuters poll of 101 economists on Nov. 6 to 10 expects 25 bps in December, to 4.00%-4.25%. Of those, 56 see December as the final hike, while 45 expect at least two further increases in total, including December.

Even a 25 bps move could lift yields and tighten financial conditions if the Fed signals more increases in 2027 than markets expect. For Bitcoin, the risk is not just December's hike size, but a shift toward higher rates for longer. Cross-market moves on Nov. 10 illustrated that pressure.

The two-year Treasury yield rose about 19 bps to 4.74%, the 10-year neared 4.99%, the dollar index gained 0.6%, and gold fell more than 2%.

Farside Investors shows US spot Bitcoin ETF net outflows of about $1.5 billion from Nov. 2 to Nov. 9.

Glassnode's Nov. 4 report found about 38% of short-term holder supply in profit, though much of the leverage built since August had cleared and spot volume ran about 1.5 times its pre-rally level.

The leverage reset may reduce the risk of another forced-selling cascade, but it does not rule out further sharp losses.

Daily net flows across US spot Bitcoin ETFs. Trading sessions from Nov. 2-Nov. 9 sum to negative $1.5 billion.

Source: Farside Investors — Bitcoin ETF daily total net flows

In its Nov. 4 report, the firm identified a modeled long-liquidation band at $73,000 to $76,000. These are estimated levels where leveraged positions could be forced to close, not confirmed buying support.

Measured from $73,000, a move to $70,000 would represent a further 4.1% decline; the round number alone does not establish technical support.

Glassnode also identified $60,000 to $63,000, 17.8% to 13.7% below, as a concentration of modeled Bitcoin long liquidations left by a breakout. That liquidation estimate is not a price forecast or a worst-case floor, and the report does not tie it to a particular Fed decision.

If core PCE cools toward 0.2% month over month, hiring slows, and officials favor quarter-point steps, a 25 bps December increase with a softer outlook would become more plausible. Bitcoin could then recover toward the $75,700 to $77,700 cost-basis levels in Glassnode's Nov. 4 report, provided ETF selling eases.

If inflation stays sticky and employment holds up, a quarter-point increase accompanied by signals of further tightening in 2027 could keep pressure on Bitcoin. The precise downside would also depend on ETF flows, spot demand and leverage.

Continued ETF outflows, weak spot demand and forced selling could deepen a decline, especially if markets price in more aggressive tightening. But neither the liquidation map nor the Fed outlook establishes that Bitcoin will reach $60,000 to $63,000.

The readings to watch are PCE on Nov. 25, jobs on Dec. 4, and the tone of the Fed before its blackout.

#5088. Can crypto trust firms compete with banks in US derivatives settlements?

Crypto custodians are moving into a market handling billions in daily settlements, but regulatory relief and a shift in congressional control leave questions over their long-term role.

Crypto companies are moving deeper into financial services traditionally dominated by banks, seeking a role in US derivatives settlements, where billions of dollars change hands daily.

The push comes as regulators expand the services trust companies can provide, even as traditional banking groups challenge parts of the regulatory framework governing crypto firms’ expansion into banklike activities.

A Nov. 5 decision by the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) illustrates the shift, allowing derivatives clearinghouses to use trust companies for daily settlements with clearing members, a function previously restricted to banks under the agency’s regulations.

The relief could create new business opportunities for regulated crypto custodians, but its revocable nature and changes in the US political landscape raise questions about the durability of the regulatory opening.

The scale of the derivatives settlement business offers some perspective on the opportunity.

CME Group, one of the world’s largest derivatives market operators, transferred an average of $8.7 billion per business day through its clearing systems during the first half of 2026, up from $6.7 billion a day in 2025, according to regulatory filings.

Those figures represent funds transferred between clearing firms to settle gains and losses, rather than fees earned by settlement banks. They nevertheless illustrate the scale of financial flows supported by the infrastructure that trust companies could increasingly help provide.

The CFTC’s Division of Clearing and Risk issued no-action relief on Nov. 5 following an Oct. 29 request from derivatives clearinghouse Nodal Clear, allowing clearinghouses to use trust companies for daily settlements if they meet the same evaluation and monitoring requirements applied to traditional settlement banks.

The decision addresses an inconsistency between federal commodities law, which permits customer funds to be held at banks or trust companies, and CFTC Regulation 39.14, which defines settlement banks only as banks.

Nodal Clear partnered with Coinbase in 2025 to integrate USDC into US futures trading. Coinbase Custody Trust was already expected to provide daily settlement services under the arrangement, Cointelegraph has learned.

Related: CFTC expands regulatory relief for passive trading software providers

Other crypto firms have also expanded their institutional financial services. On Nov. 4, BitGo launched its Global Markets division, combining derivatives trading, financing, collateral management and settlement capabilities.

Coinbase’s national trust bank charter also took effect on Nov. 6, allowing Coinbase National Trust Company, a separate entity from Coinbase Custody Trust, to provide federally supervised digital asset custody services.

Several major crypto firms have also secured US national trust bank approvals over the past months, including Circle, Ripple, Crypto.com and Stripe-owned Bridge. The charters, granted by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC), reflect the industry’s expansion into federally regulated financial services.

The expansion of specialized trust companies has prompted objections from traditional banking groups.

In October, the Independent Community Bankers of America sued the OCC, arguing that its national trust bank charter rules could allow crypto firms to conduct certain banklike activities without meeting all the requirements imposed on conventional banks.

The lawsuit reflects a broader disagreement over how specialized financial institutions should be regulated as they expand into services also provided by banks.

Cody Carbone, CEO of The Digital Chamber, told Cointelegraph that the CFTC’s relief does not put trust companies in a better or worse position than traditional banks.

Trust companies and banks already compete for services permitted under their respective charters, Carbone said, adding that the decision gives clearinghouses more options without preventing banks from retaining their business. “A trust company charter is not a regulatory shortcut,” he said.

Despite the potential opportunities, the CFTC’s decision leaves questions about how securely trust companies can establish a long-term role in derivatives settlement.

The agency issued a no-action letter rather than formally amending its regulations. The letter states that CFTC staff will not recommend enforcement action against clearinghouses using trust companies for daily settlements if specified conditions are met.

Unlike a formal rule amendment, which generally requires a public notice-and-comment process, staff no-action relief does not change the underlying regulation and can be withdrawn.

“A change in Commission leadership could withdraw the CFTC Letter. More formal Commission action, such as amending the regulation, would be necessary to avoid this potential risk,” Jordan Yeagley, a shareholder at Buchanan Ingersoll & Rooney, told Cointelegraph.

The uncertainty comes at a politically significant moment for the agency. CFTC Chair Michael Selig remains the regulator’s sole commissioner, despite a statutory structure providing for five commissioners. Selig has led the agency since December 2025, leaving four seats vacant as the regulator expands its work on digital asset markets.

The political environment is also changing following the Nov. 3 US midterm elections, in which Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress.

The incoming Congress, which takes office in January, will have greater power to scrutinize the CFTC’s regulatory agenda. The Senate also has a role in confirming nominees to fill the agency’s vacant commissioner seats, while the House and Senate Agriculture Committees oversee the regulator.

Sen. Sherrod Brown, who won Ohio’s Senate race on Nov. 3 despite more than $60 million in opposition spending from crypto-backed political action committees, could take on a prominent role on the Senate Agriculture Committee.

Magazine: What Democrats’ US midterm sweep means for the CLARITY Act
