#4978. Prisoner Jailed for 8 Years Over $2.7M Crypto Theft Plot Run From His Cell

Prisoner Jailed for 8 Years Over $2.7M Crypto Theft Plot Run From His Cell

A man who directed an attempted £2 million ($2.7 million) cryptocurrency theft from his prison cell has been jailed for eight years by Scotland's High Court of Justiciary.

Robert Barr, 29, pleaded guilty along with Sean Favier, 26, and Iain Doyle, 37, to being involved in serious organised crime as part of the attempted theft, according to a sentencing statement published by Scotland's Judicial Office on Tuesday. Lord Cubie sentenced Favier to four years and Doyle to five years at the High Court in Glasgow.

"This was a crime which married 21st century computer sophistication with old fashioned housebreaking in an audacious attempt to steal cryptocurrency worth up to £2 million pounds," Lord Cubie said.

The plan, according to the court's account, was for Favier and Doyle to get into the victims' home in Midlothian, near Edinburgh, and make the man go onto his computer and download AnyDesk, a remote-access program. That would let Barr, who was serving a 21-month sentence at HMP Edinburgh for fraud, take control of the computer from prison and transfer the crypto to wallets he controlled.

The two men went to the house at around 11:30 p.m. on April 17, 2024, but the couple saw them on their doorbell camera and refused to let them in. One of the men looked into the lens and asked whether there was a camera in front of him before the pair left, the BBC reported.

The attempt followed months of preparation, including identifying the victim as a crypto holder, gathering images of the house and reconnaissance, all of which investigators pieced together from Barr's phone after it was seized in June 2024, the judge said. Messages on the phone "plainly contemplated threats and/or violence," he added.

The victim was a co-founder of a crypto startup who did not advertise his holdings, but followed mostly crypto-related accounts on social media, according to the BBC. Barr's associates had already burgled the house in January 2024 and taken a crypto wallet, but could not access the funds without its PIN, the court heard.

Prosecutors described Barr as a "sophisticated cybercriminal" able to "orchestrate a level of criminality rarely seen within mainstream investigations." He had a previous conviction for a crypto fraud, and organised the new offence while in prison "using plainly illicit means of being in contact or online," Lord Cubie said, calling those materially aggravating factors.

The judge said that the plot was only frustrated because the victims "had the sense not to open their front door" to unknown men with their faces covered. Lord Cubie will consider a serious crime prevention order against Barr on November 17. Barr is also in custody awaiting potential extradition, the judge noted.

Had the men got inside, the plot would likely have escalated into a so-called wrench attack, in which criminals use force or the threat of it to make crypto holders hand over their funds. Such attacks are on pace for a record year, with more than $30 million stolen in the first half of 2026, according to a Chainalysis report.

The UK has seen several recent cases. Three teenagers were jailed earlier this year after stealing $4.3 million in crypto at knifepoint, and three men were sentenced last month over a failed home invasion aimed at stealing crypto. In September, Crimestoppers offered a reward of up to £10,000 for information on an attack on a UK couple in which, police believe, a fourth man relayed instructions to the three attackers over a video call.

#4976. Crypto fraudster jailed for eight years over $2.7 million theft plot directed from prison

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The plot was foiled after the would-be accomplices failed to gain entry to the victims’ home, while Barr’s phone later exposed his role.

A Scottish court sentenced Robert Barr to eight years' imprisonment for orchestrating an attempted £2 million ($2.7 million) cryptocurrency theft from his prison cell at HMP Edinburgh.

Barr was serving a 21-month sentence for cryptocurrency-related fraud when he organized the operation. The High Court in Glasgow sentenced him alongside Sean Favier, who received four years, and Iain Doyle, who was jailed for five years.

The trio pleaded guilty to involvement in serious organized crime prior to their sentencing, the court said in a statement on Tuesday.

According to the court's account, the plan relied on AnyDesk, software that allows users to access and control another computer remotely. Barr wanted his accomplices to get inside the victims' home and make the homeowner download the program, allowing him to control the couple's computer remotely from prison.

Once connected, Barr planned to enter a receiving cryptocurrency address and move funds from the victims' wallet into accounts under his control.

However, the plan didn't get that far. The court heard that Favier and Doyle arrived at a couple's home at about 11:30 p.m. on April 17, 2024, but the occupants spotted them on their doorbell camera and refused to open the door. The pair left without gaining entry.

"This was a crime which married 21st-century computer sophistication with old-fashioned housebreaking," Lord Cubie said when sentencing the men.

The court was told that investigators uncovered Barr's failed heist after seizing his phone on June 25, 2024 and finding messages detailing his role in and instructions to Favier and Doyle.

Addressing Barr's previous conviction on crypto-related fraud, the judge said his decision to organize another offence from prison was a "material aggravating factor."

"In terms of the sentence to be imposed if you had been found guilty after trial, I would have imposed a sentence of eight years and ten months," the judge said.

Barr faces a serious crime prevention hearing on Nov. 17.

#4974. Bitcoin Core discloses three patched security flaws, including high-severity mining bug

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Thousands of Bitcoin nodes granted trusted permissions to incoming Tor connections under certain configurations, according to one of the advisories.

Bitcoin Core publicly disclosed three security vulnerabilities on Tuesday, including a high-severity flaw that could prevent affected mining nodes from creating valid block templates.

The earliest of the three was reported in April 2025, with the other two reported in September last year. Bitcoin Core, the software used by many Bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) nodes, issued fixes for each of them in October 2025.

The high-severity CVE-2025-62861 affected Bitcoin Core versions 28.0 to 28.2 and 29.0 to 29.1. Niklas Goegge reported it on Sept. 26, 2025. Bitcoin Core's TRUC "sibling eviction" rules were still being applied to transactions returning to the mempool after a chain reorganization. The bug could evict a transaction without removing its descendants, leaving the mempool inconsistent. Miners affected by the issue had to restart their nodes before they could create valid block templates again.

Eugene Siegel reported the medium-severity CVE-2025-62859 issue in April 2025. The vulnerability meant a malicious peer could exploit witness malleation in compact blocks to delay block processing by up to 10 minutes. The advisory said the attack could be repeated indefinitely against nodes running in "-blocksonly" mode, or where the attacker controlled all three high-bandwidth peers.

Bitcoin Core 30.0 shipped with fixes for all three vulnerabilities on Oct. 10, 2025, followed by version 29.2 on Oct. 14, 2025. The last vulnerable Bitcoin Core version went end of life on Oct. 27 this year, according to the advisories.

In May, The Block reported on a separate high-severity memory safety vulnerability that developers had also patched before publicly disclosing it.

#4973. Avalanche Won Over the NYSE. Now It Has to ‘Capture Value’ for AVAX

Avalanche Won Over the NYSE. Now It Has to ‘Capture Value’ for AVAX

The New York Stock Exchange this week selected Avalanche to provide infrastructure for a tokenized securities platform it's prepping. Experts say that it's a big deal for Avalanche's reputation, but warn that it may not translate into an uptick in value for the network's AVAX token.

On Monday, Ava Labs announced Avalanche would serve as the "core ledger technology" for a tokenized securities platform developed by NYSE parent company Intercontinental Exchange. The product will be designed to allow 24/7 trading, clearing, and settlement of tokenized securities, subject to regulatory approval.

Carlos Guzman, research analyst at market maker GSR, told Decrypt it’s “definitely a big win for the project.”

“Having NYSE choose its tech for settlement is a big institutional credibility boost, and it validates the benefits blockchain tech can provide to traditional financial plumbing with instant settlement and programmability,” he said.

Luther Maday, head of North America at crypto payments firm Mercuryo, told Decrypt that the decision came after a year of testing on Avalanche, which signals the network passed checks on "performance, reliability, and control."

"NYSE has chosen the technology for the clearing and settlement layer, which is a much deeper use of blockchain than simply issuing a tokenized asset on a network," Maday added.

Avalanche's AVAX token has risen 3.6% in the past day to $8.64, according to Decrypt data. But experts warn that providing infrastructure doesn't necessarily mean a long-term increase in token value.

"NYSE will be using Avalanche’s infrastructure for clearing and settlement, so the actual trading will still happen off-chain," Guzman explained.

This means NYSE activity will run on dedicated Avalanche infrastructure rather than the public C-Chain, where most of the network's activity happens.

"I would be careful about translating the news directly into expectations for activity on the public C-Chain," Maday said.

That said, Maday said to keep an eye on the details of how the AVAX token is used within the dedicated infrastructure. He also suggested that stablecoin liquidity, validator activity, and network fees could see a boost.

The NYSE has also teamed up with Blockchain.com to sell tokenized stocks and ETFs to crypto investors, while its parent company ICE is working with OKX on a 24/7 tokenized stock venue under an SEC exemption. Morgan Stanley, meanwhile, has started tokenizing securities of its own.

Not every company wants in, though. All seven of the so-called "Magnificent Seven" stocks—Apple, Meta, and the like—opted out of having their shares tokenized on the OKX-ICE venue, along with dozens of other companies.

Even if tokenization does take off, that doesn't guarantee profits for AVAX investors.

"The main question for Avalanche is whether it can do what other blockchains have struggled to do: capture value," Misha Putiatin, founder of collateral markets platform Symbiotic, told Decrypt. "Avalanche has always had an unusually outcome-focused team for this industry, so I suspect they have plans for that, but it remains to be seen."

Ava Labs did not immediately respond to Decrypt's request for comment.

#4969. OKXICE sets Nov. 23 launch for tokenized securities venue under SEC innovation exemption

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

On Wednesday, OKX and ICE announced it would launch its TSV on Nov. 23 as part of the SEC's five-year innovation exemption.

OKXICE, the joint venture between OKX and NYSE parent Intercontinental Exchange, plans to launch its tokenized securities venue at the end of the month, bringing the Securities and Exchange Commission's "innovation exemption" to life.

On Wednesday, OKX and ICE announced it would launch its TSV on Nov. 23 as part of the SEC's five-year innovation exemption that debuted in September to bring capital markets in the U.S. on-chain and not have to register as more traditional exchanges.

"The future of finance is onchain, and we have an opportunity to build it here in America, with the standards, safeguards and ambition that investors expect,” said former New York Governor and Co-Chair of OKXICE Andrew Cuomo, in a statement.

The announcement of the launch comes amid dozens of issuers that say they have objected to tokenized versions of their stock trading on OKXICE. As part of the innovation exemption, issuers can decide that they don't want their stock to trade on certain venues. In the latest filing from Nov. 5, 67 companies had opted out, including Meta Platforms, Inc. The Home Depot, Netflix, Tesla, and Costco.

On Wednesday, ICE and OKX said it has confirmed 70 assets so far from issuers including Coinbase, Circle and Robinhood, as well as 59 exchange-traded funds. The OKXICE's TSV is available to customers in the U.S. who have a CeDeFi Wallet and have completed onboarding, the companies said.

A few reasons have since been reported for why companies objected to trading on OKXICE. A spokesperson for one of the companies that opted out, Applied Optoelectronics, told the Wall Street Journal the fiber-optic maker objected because it was unsure how tokenization would affect its stock price, liquidity and volatility, and also had concerns about whether investors may be confused about the company's involvement in the offering.

Another major exchange, Nasdaq is also working on tokenization and has partnered with crypto exchange Kraken on it. In late September, the exchange posted a Q&A on the SEC's innovation exemption. Nasdaq has taken a different view in that it has pushed back against the SEC's innovation exemption and has said it plans to "opt its own stock out of Tokenized Securities Venues," said Nasdaq Chief Legal and Regulatory Officer John Zecca in an op-ed published by Barron's on Tuesday.

Zecca said tokenization can be a positive for share issuers, but said a framework for tokenization should protect the rights attached to the underlying shares, uphold the principle that a tokenized stock remains a stock, and ensure issuers have a meaningful role in shaping how their shares are tokenized.

"Our greater concern, however, is that the Innovation Exemption allows tokenized securities to trade outside the government-regulated National Market System, which maintains a market data, reporting, and transparency framework that all stock trading must abide by," Zecca said, adding that fragmentation could then occur.

#4968. Nvidia Sheds $430B in Two Days as Jensen Huang Faces Calls to Resign

Nvidia Sheds $430B in Two Days as Jensen Huang Faces Calls to Resign

Nvidia has lost about $430 billion in market value in two days, as pressure grows in Washington over CEO Jensen Huang's ties to a businesswoman U.S. intelligence reportedly concluded was a Chinese spy.

Nvidia shares closed down 5.5% at $168.89 on Tuesday, following a 4.3% drop on Monday, according to Yahoo! Finance. That leaves the stock down about 9.5% since Friday's close, wiping out roughly $430 billion in market value, according to Yahoo! Finance data. The sell-off, which saw shares slide as much as 8% on Monday, has wiped out all of Nvidia's gains for 2026. The shares were up 3.8% at $175.23 in premarket trading early on Wednesday.

The independent members of Nvidia's board have retained Joon Kim, a former acting U.S. Attorney for the Southern District of New York, and his firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to carry out the investigation it announced on Sunday, the company said on Tuesday.

A new statement from the independent members of the NVIDIA Board: https://t.co/uSFDhiohG8

— NVIDIA Newsroom (@nvidianewsroom) November 10, 2026

Sen. Kevin Cramer, a North Dakota Republican on the Senate Banking Committee, called on Huang to step down, telling Politico late Tuesday that the risk of extortion was too great. "The potential for extortion is so tremendous that something as sensitive as Nvidia chips, I just think he's got to go," Cramer said, adding that the board's internal investigation was "fine, but not nearly enough."

Hours earlier, former National Economic Council director Larry Kudlow had urged Huang to resign on his Fox Business show. "Step down, Mr. Huang, right now," Kudlow said, adding that he was "not here to indict" Huang but that he should step aside while the investigations take place.

Cramer's committee has jurisdiction over bills that would curb Nvidia chip sales to China, including the AI Overwatch Act and the Chip Security Act, which are being considered for inclusion in the annual defense spending bill, Politico reported.

Senate Democratic leader Chuck Schumer, meanwhile, accused President Donald Trump of failing to act after being briefed on the relationship. "What a dereliction of duty," Schumer said on the Senate floor on Tuesday, according to CNBC. "He's supposed to protect America, not China, not his friends."

Rep. John Moolenaar (R-MI), who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said he wanted to know whether Huang shared privileged information with Alice Huang and whether he was influenced to lobby against chip export restrictions. "If it becomes less of an investigation, more of damage control, that's not going to work," Moolenaar told CNBC. House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast told ABC News he was prepared to subpoena Huang unless he responded by Wednesday to a request for a closed-door briefing on November 18, the day after Nvidia reports quarterly earnings.

Not everyone in Washington wants the scandal to dominate. Rep. Jay Obernolte (R-CA), co-chair of the House AI task force, told CNBC that a personal scandal should not "interfere" with the policy debate over chip exports to China.

Trump has said the FBI is looking into the matter, and that Huang told him he had no idea the woman was a spy.

Alice Huang, who also goes by Huang Le and is not related to Jensen Huang, worked in banking and investment management in China before becoming the controlling shareholder of Singapore-based Megaspeed in March 2023, according to a Reuters review of corporate records and filings.

Megaspeed, which Nvidia lists as a cloud partner, has drawn U.S. scrutiny over whether it helped Chinese firms obtain export-controlled Nvidia chips, but there has been no public finding that it broke the rules, Reuters reported.

China has denied the intelligence allegations, and Reuters said it could not independently verify them. George Chen, a partner at consultancy The Asia Group, told the news agency that Jensen Huang "may also be viewed as a potential victim" if he was targeted without knowing who she was.

#4967. The Best Foot Baths for Soothing Tired Feet, According to Podiatrists

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

As a longtime distance runner, my feet can get pretty roughed up after a grueling workout—even with the proper running shoes. But since I don’t have the time or endless funds to regularly hoof it to my local spa for a foot massage or pedicure, a home foot bath is my go-to. The best foot baths allow you to soak your tired toes, with extra features like bubbles, jets, and massage functions to help your feet feel rejuvenated.

A good foot soak at the end of a long day or workout can do wonders for your mental health, too, ultimately supporting your overall health. But unsurprisingly, a lot of the potential perks from home foot baths revolve around your feet. These baths can help boost circulation in your feet and combat inflammation that could lead to pain, swelling, and overuse injuries if it’s left unchecked, says Bradley Schaeffer, DPM, a podiatrist and foot surgeon specializing in sports medicine in New York City. Plus, now that we’re at peak cozy season, shopping for a great foot bath for yourself or a loved one (it’s a great gift for her!) is a cost-effective way to give your feet some R&R.

Doing foot baths a few times a week can help to slough off dead skin that builds up quickly, leaving you with softer, less grody-looking feet, says Melissa Lockwood, DPM, a podiatrist at Heartland Foot and Ankle Associates in Bloomington, Illinois

Not all foot baths are created equal, and it’s important to choose one based on your needs. So, if bubbles make you feel uncomfortable (me, hi), a foot bath that simply swirls around warm water may be more your speed. We also tapped Schaeffer, Lockwood, and Suzanne C. Fuchs, DPM, a podiatrist at Luxe Podiatry in Jupiter, Florida, for their advice on what to look for when shopping for these foot health-supportive devices and to get their feedback on their favorites.

Using that guidance and our personal experience, we found factors like size, weight, and additional features matter when choosing a foot bath. At baseline, a foot bath is a container to hold water to soothe your feet. But there are plenty of specific features to keep in mind when shopping for a foot bath.

The Hospan Collapsible Foot Spa packs a lot of features into a device you can fold flat. A huge highlight is the eight groups of massaging balls that go to work to ease pressure on the bottoms of your feet. (These aren’t motorized, but you can run your feet over them for a soothing effect.) While it’s a good idea to start out with warm or hot water, the Hospan Collapsible Foot Spa has temperature adjustment, allowing you to ramp up the heat between 95 and 118 degrees Fahrenheit without having to toss in more liquid. An LED display keeps tabs on the water temperature, giving you real-time information on how hot it is.

As the name suggests, this foot bath is collapsible and drops down when it’s not in use, making it easy to slide into or under tight spaces. A few potential downsides to consider: Hospan’s bubbles aren’t the strongest you’ll find, and this is a pricier bath when it’s not on sale, although it’s usually marked down a few times a year. But if you’re looking to pamper your feet with a range of options and are okay with investing a little extra, this is a solid choice that will hold up well and does just about everything.

It’s hard to find a foot bath that checks all the boxes, but the Conair Soothing Pedicure Foot Spa Bath comes really close. This bath allows you to get a good soak with a few extras to take your experience to a new level. “This model features massage settings, which can enhance relaxation,” Fuchs says.

This bath has a modest price tag of $30, which is significantly less than some others you’ll come across. It also uses vibration for a massage and has a pinpoint massage tool in the center in case you want to work on a particular area of your foot. While there’s no bubble feature, not everyone is into bubbles (they tickle my feet, which isn’t my favorite).

Conair’s foot bath is easy to use and clean, and offers plenty of opportunities to pamper your feet. The Conair Soothing Pedicure Foot Spa Bath uses a toe-touch control to turn the vibration settings on and off, although there’s no remote. This bath isn’t powerful enough to heat up cool or room temperature water, but it does a solid job of keeping already-warm liquid at the same temperature.

Not every foot bath has a bubble feature, and some are stronger than others. If bubbles are a priority for you, Fuchs recommends the Homedics Smart Space Elite Foot Bath. “Known for its bubbling action, this foot spa provides a soothing experience,” she says.

The bath delivers plenty of bubbles, along with heat, to create a spa-like feel while you soak. It also has three pedicure attachments that pop into the center: a massager, brush, and pumice stone. Those attachments neatly fit into on-board storage when they’re not being used. An ergonomic footbed with massaging bristles on the bottom of the bath also helps cushion your soles while you rest. The whole thing folds up pretty small, and has a special locking system for when it’s filled up.

If you’re interested in a foot bath that will give you a shiatsu-type massage, this isn’t it. But if you love the idea of blissfully scrolling on your phone while your feet are surrounded by warm, bubbly goodness, the Homedics Smart Space Elite Foot Bath is the right choice.

Getting the temperature just right in your foot bath can be tricky. But the Sharper Image Spa Haven Foot Bath has temperature control, allowing you to punch in the level of heat you want up to 108 degrees and let it go to work for you. This foot bath is loaded with features, including separate bubble and heat options, and free-spinning massaging rollers on the bottom that allow you to control the pressure and speed. (The rollers are removable for cleaning.)

The bubbling action is delivered in your choice of four speeds, and there’s also a removable pumice stone to help get rid of calluses and dead skin. When you’re done with your foot bath, just open the drain plug to let the water seep out. The only major downside to this foot bath (besides the high price) is that it’s not collapsible. But that also means it’s sturdy enough to rest your feet on the side when you want to take a break—a feature you won’t find in baths that break down.

The Medical King Foot Spa has six sets of massaging rollers to help knead out stress and pain after a long day. Couple that with bubble jets, vibration settings, and a built-in heater, and you’ve got a recipe for a good time.

This relatively affordable foot bath comes with a remote control that doubles as a control panel, with clear buttons to adjust the massage mode and flip through three temperature settings as you kick back. You can also set a timer to cut off the features whenever you want. The foot bath is collapsible, so storage under your bed or couch shouldn’t be an issue. This foot bath delivers if you’re looking for a way to work out kinks in your soles without having to put in any major effort.

You can splash out on a bunch of different features when it comes to a foot bath, but sometimes you just want a standard soak. If that’s the case—and you want to save some cash, too—consider the Mantello Foot Bath. This barebones bath is as basic as they come, but it has a few features that elevate it above your standard plastic bucket.

The bath has a sturdy plastic design that won’t tip over easily. It’s also deep, giving you plenty of space to soak your calves, too. The base has built-in arch support and raised dots for a little something extra while your feet relax. The open design means it’s easy to clean, too. There are no bubbles, massagers, or built-in heaters. But for a mere $23, what do you expect? If you’re just looking to unwind with a solid soak, this is a great choice.

“Foot baths can offer several benefits for foot health,” Fuchs says. She lists off a bunch of potential perks, including helping your muscles (and mind) relax, improved circulation, removing the buildup of dirt and bacteria, soothing conditions like athlete’s foot or plantar fasciitis, and exfoliation. Of course, you don’t have to use a foot bath to have good foot health—but many people find that it can be a comfortable way to support it.

Should you use Epsom salts in your foot bath?

In case you’re not familiar with them, Epsom salts can help soften skin and support muscle relaxation. Fuchs recommends adding Epsom salts or essential oils to your tub to ramp up the relaxation and health perks.

The odds are good that you’ll be hauling a foot bath in and out of a storage spot on a regular basis. Most foot baths aren’t overly heavy, but some higher-tech options have more heft to them than others. (The baths we recommend range from 1 pound to 9 pounds.) Just take a peek at the weight to make sure you can comfortably carry your device around.

If your feet are on the larger side, you want to make sure there’s enough room for them to fit comfortably inside your bath. Also, that foot bath has to go somewhere, both when it’s put away and in use. Check the dimensions and compare them to where you plan to use your bath, along with the drawer or closet space where you plan to stash it, to make sure you have enough room.

This came up with every podiatrist we spoke to. “Feet can get gross and we want to be able to have a sanitized bath each time we use it,” Lockwood says. Schaeffer agrees. “Bacteria can grow in these, raising the risk of infections,” he points out. With that, it’s crucial to look for a foot bath that’s easy to clean. That usually means the bath can be disassembled for scrub-downs or at least have very detailed cleaning instructions.

This isn’t a requirement, but having the option to collapse your foot bath can be helpful if you’re short on storage space. Many collapsible foot baths can fold down relatively flat, and have locking mechanisms to keep them in place when they’re in use. But if you have the room, you might prefer the sturdier design of a non-collapsible foot bath.

Many foot baths have features to give you more than a good soak, like bubbles, jets, and massagers. Some will heat up the water for you, and others have helpful elements like remote control, rollers, and pumice stones. These extra features usually come with a higher price, so consider what you will and won’t use if you’re looking to save on a bath.

To choose the best foot baths, I first relied on my own personal experience with these tools, making sure to weed out options I’ve used that have leaked, broken, or just not lived up to the hype. From there, I reached out to three podiatrists located across the country for their feedback on what to look for while shopping for these baths. I also utilized in-house testing by Women’s Health editors for units we got feet into. Each foot bath was evaluated on size, weight, ease of cleaning, and extra features like bubbles and built-in massagers to create the final list. Keeping all of that in mind, these foot baths are worth snapping up to get your soak on.

She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#4966. Nomura’s Laser Digital helps launch Saudi SME credit vault targeting $100 million on BNB Chain

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The partnership plans to extend its model to other emerging markets and credit strategies after launching its first Saudi private credit product.

Nomura's digital assets subsidiary Laser Digital is expanding its tokenization push into Saudi Arabian private credit, with partner ZIG Finance launching an onchain investment vehicle targeting a $100 million raise.

Dubbed the zEMS Vault, the product gives eligible institutional investors exposure to a diversified pool of Saudi small and medium-sized enterprise loans originated through Beehive's lending infrastructure, according to a statement shared with The Block.

ZIG Finance said Laser Digital will serve as the vault's risk framework advisor, helping design its risk governance structure and providing tokenization advisory.

"The aim isn't to remove credit risk. It's to make that risk transparent and manageable," ZIG Finance co-founder Abdul Rafay Gadit said. "We see this vault as a way to open a deeper pool of institutional capital to Saudi private credit, with the potential to build on that model across other emerging markets.”

The zEMS Vault is the first product under the strategic partnership between ZIG Finance and Laser Digital. The companies intend to apply the same structure to other emerging markets and credit strategies, pairing local lenders with institutional risk governance, compliant tokenization, and onchain distribution. Further opportunities across the region and other asset classes are already under consideration, the statement said.

Meanwhile, the launch adds to Laser Digital's institutional investment product business, which includes a tokenized Bitcoin Diversified Yield Fund launched in January through KAIO.

The firm also received conditional approval from the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency in May to establish a national trust bank, subject to pre-opening requirements. Its proposed services include stablecoin intermediation, collateral management, and multi-asset custody.

#4965. Kraken Parent Payward Brings 200 UK and EU Stocks Onchain as xStocks

Kraken Parent Payward Brings 200 UK and EU Stocks Onchain as xStocks

Payward, the parent company of crypto exchange Kraken, has tokenized more than 200 UK and European stocks through its xStocks platform, it announced on Wednesday.

The first batch covers some of the most actively traded companies listed in the UK and the European Union, with more to be added on a rolling basis. Like other xStocks, each token is backed 1:1 by the underlying share, which is bought and held by a regulated custodian for as long as the token trades, according to Payward.

Payward's @xStocksFi is expanding beyond US tokenized equities.

In partnership with @GTNglobal, more than 200 of the UK and EU’s largest equities are now tokenized by xStocks, including HSBC, Shell, LVMH and Airbus.

The first international launch, with more markets to follow.…

The tokens are available to trade on Kraken and through other platforms in the xStocks Alliance, and on blockchains where xStocks already trade, including Solana, Ethereum and TON. They are offered in more than 110 markets, but remain unavailable to investors in the UK.

"A portfolio should not have borders or trading hours," said Mark Greenberg, Payward's chief commercial officer. "Tokenizing every other international market is the next step."

The launch takes xStocks past 1,500 tokenized stocks, ETFs and IPOs. The platform holds more than $1.1 billion in assets, has processed over $45 billion in total transaction volume, and has more than 550,000 holders, Payward said.

The new tokens are the first delivered under Payward's partnership with GTN, a brokerage infrastructure firm that handles execution and custody of the underlying shares. When the two partnered in July, Payward said it planned to start with Hong Kong-listed stocks; hundreds of Hong Kong listings will now follow in the coming weeks, the company said.

The expansion also delivers on part of Payward's partnership with the London Stock Exchange, announced in September, under which Payward agreed to tokenize the 100 largest London-listed companies. Subject to regulatory approval, the exchange intends to list xStocks and begin trading them on LSE 24, its planned near-24-hour trading venue, in 2027.

Payward has been drawing interest from traditional exchanges. Nasdaq agreed to invest $100 million in the company at a $21 billion valuation in September, building on a March partnership to bring tokenized stocks to market in 2027, while Deutsche Börse bought a $200 million stake in April.

The company has also been expanding through acquisitions, agreeing to buy derivatives exchange Bitnomial for $550 million and Lithuania's Saldo Bank as part of a push into the EU.

#4964. FSB urgest Turkey to tighten crypto oversight, set stablecoin rules

The Financial Stability Board cited progress on crypto licensing and urged stronger enforcement and cooperation with foreign regulators.

The Financial Stability Board (FSB) urged Turkey to tighten its oversight of crypto markets and adopt dedicated rules for stablecoins.

In a peer review published on Wednesday, the global financial watchdog said Turkish authorities had made progress by introducing registration and licensing requirements for crypto service providers.

The report was part of the FSB’s regular program of country reviews and assessed Turkey’s progress in implementing the board’s 2023 global recommendations on crypto and stablecoins. It followed a broader 2025 review that found gaps in how jurisdictions had implemented those recommendations.

The FSB recommended that Turkey begin monitoring crypto-related risks to financial stability under a formal framework, strengthen supervision and enforcement, and improve cooperation and information sharing with foreign regulators.

Turkey’s Capital Markets Board introduced licensing and operational requirements for crypto exchanges and custodians in March 2025, including minimum capital thresholds.

The FSB said Turkey had introduced safeguards including requirements to segregate assets held in custody and a ban on domestic crypto payments. The safeguards help limit the spread of crypto-related risks to banks and payment systems. Authorities also launched a centralized platform to monitor crypto activities.

The FSB’s 2025 review found that Turkey had finalized rules for crypto assets but lacked a dedicated stablecoin framework.

In June 2025, Turkey’s Finance Ministry outlined plans to introduce additional transaction checks and limits on stablecoin transfers to combat money laundering.

#4963. The Best Travel Backpacks for Women in 2026, Tested by Editors

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

The right travel backpack helps you master the art of packing light, so you aren’t hauling unnecessary weight to Terminal B. It has enough space for your flight essentials—laptop, passport, water bottle, charging cables—and is comfortable enough to strap on for hours. Whether you need an upgrade to complement your new carry-on, are investing in a pack to carry you through your next adventure, or simply looking for a great Christmas gift, here’s what to consider before you shop.

Below, see the best travel backpacks for women to take on your next adventure.

Topo’s Rover Pack is truly multifunctional, and that’s why we’ve dubbed it our best overall travel backpack. Its thoughtful design, roomy compartments, and casual look make it great for someone who’s always on the move, whether they’re going to the gym, commuting to work, or packing for their next trip.

The backpack has a 20-liter capacity, spread across a large main compartment and a zippered pocket on the front. There’s also a padded internal laptop sleeve, two adjustable water bottle pockets, and more. Plus, it’s made with dual side compression and spacer mesh shoulder straps. While it packs a ton, the bag is midsize enough that it can be used as an everyday bag, too.

The backpack’s main compartment is designed with a drawstring closure that can be cinched closed, and then secured using the two exterior straps with buckle closures. This adds some reassurance that whatever you’ve packed inside won’t spill out, or be at risk of getting stolen on hectic travel days.

We love that this bag comes in more than 15 fun color combinations that will stand out from the rest of the plain backpacks. Bonus points that it’s made from durable recycled nylon.

Most of the best travel backpacks will cost you at least $100—and sometimes more. But the Matein Carry-On Travel Backpack is the exception. This bag is designed with the same features as the other backpacks in this guide for a fraction of the price—under $40. It has more than seven compartments, including a hidden pocket with two ventilation holes that can be used to store shoes up to a size 12 or dirty laundry.

Inside you’ll find a padded neoprene pocket that can fit laptops up to 17 inches. The pocket closes with two zippers that meet, allowing you to add a lock to that particular compartment if you want to deter thieves. One feature we particularly love (in addition to its multiple compartments) is that it’s designed with comfort in mind. The part of the backpack that lies on your lower back when worn is made with extra padding to avoid discomfort, and its straps are padded, too.

A chest strap with a buckle closure would have made this backpack perfect to help distribute some of its weight (40 liters can feel quite heavy to some!), but it does come with a luggage strap you can use to rest it on your carry-on or checked luggage while you’re on your way to your destination.

We also love the hidden pocket in the back to store your most valuable items, like money, your passport, or other documents. To pack the main compartment, you can open the bag up like a clamshell, which makes it easy to store your clothing and other travel essentials in a way that makes the most out of the space you have.

The North Face Borealis is a classic backpack that’s been around for decades—25 years to be exact, and many people who own the cult-favorite bag will tell you they’ve had one for years. The Borealis was originally designed to be used on the trails, but it’s so spacious and easy to organize that it has gained a reputation as a commuter and travel backpack as well.

Assistant social media editor Isabel McMahon says she’s been personally using this bag for years because it’s so comfortable and durable. “The padded back is essential for a long travel day,” she says. McMahon also says the bag’s internal laptop compartment (which fits a computer up to 16 inches) is “next-level,” as it sits a few inches above the bottom of the bag to protect your electronics from accidental drops. The inside also features other small pockets for a phone, documents, and other tiny valuables.

We particularly love that this backpack is designed from a recycled nylon and polyester fabric blend that’s both water-repellent and hard to rip. That’s one of the reasons the bag is so durable—it can truly last decades when cared for. The iconic bungee cord system in the front adds a stylish note that makes this backpack stand apart from other options, but the cords are also functional, too. These can be tightened to hold other essentials, like a water bottle, travel blanket, or jacket.

McMahon’s only complaint about this bag is that there are a lot of straps and clips, which can sometimes get in the way and accidentally snag onto other items, she says. Still, she raves that it makes the perfect bag for a weekend trip at 27 liters.

Another cool feature of this bag is that it’s made with The North Face’s FlexVent system, certified by the American Chiropractic Association. This means the shoulder straps and back panel are designed to contour to the curvature of your spine, so that the bag is easier to hold and there’s less of a chance of you experiencing soreness or fatigue.

Read More: Best Travel Essentials for Women

As denoted by its name, the Triple-Zip Backpack has three main zipper compartments: the main center compartment, the large front pocket, and an external padded laptop pocket that can fit up to a 16-inch laptop. Each pocket is strategically placed, so you have easy access to your laptop, hydration, and any small essentials without having to dig into the main compartment.

Lululemon’s Tech Canvas material features reflective details and is made from recycled polyester, which is water-repellent, making this backpack a great choice for travel. It also has a trolley-compatible sleeve to slip over your luggage while making your way through the airport.

We love the attention to detail built into the thick padded straps—they sit closer together on the back to evenly distribute the load and have an adjustable sternum strap. Plus, load-lifter straps secure the bag to your upper body and the cushy back panel provides lumbar support.

Hikers or outdoor adventurers will enjoy traveling with Cotopaxi’s Allpa 28L Travel Pack. It’s lightweight, made of rip-proof fabric, and easy to clean. Additionally, it’s waterproof, which also makes it a solid choice for those that will face a variety of weather conditions. The Allpa is the backpack I traveled with when I trekked through the Amazon in Ecuador for two days. While there, I regularly had to lay the backpack on the ground for rest and other reasons, so it did get quite dirty. However, I loved that all it took was a good wipe-down to make the bag look brand-new again. I love that it can be easily cleaned using a wet towel, which makes it a solid choice for trips where it can get soiled easily.

Even better? The backpack is made with 100 percent recycled fabric, but it doesn’t skimp on durability. This bag was regularly grazed by tree branches during my hikes or dragged across a floor in my quest to unpack it, and it didn’t experience any rips. It’s made with many of the same features as the other backpacks in this guide—different-sized compartments, a water bottle mesh pocket on the side, and a rollerbag sleeve for resting it on luggage—but it also features four webbing loops along each corner of the bag, which are perfect for attaching gear, like a jacket, clip-on speaker, or a flashlight.

My only gripe with the backpack is that it has so many compartments I lost track of where I put things sometimes. But now that I’ve used it several times, that’s no longer an issue. It’s something first-time users can definitely experience, though.

While the Osprey Fairview is a much pricier buy than the Matein, it’s a lot more supportive (and long-term durable), making it well worth the splurge. Meg Wubbenhorst, a travel expert who has traveled to five continents and over 40 countries, says she regularly used the 40-liter backpack during her 20 months of solo travel, even though she originally thought it looked too small.

“I was pleasantly surprised at the roominess—I sometimes felt like Mary Poppins when unpacking, with ample room for all of my clothes, shoes (no more than 3 pairs!), and various travel accessories,” she says.

From the compression straps on the outside and inside of the bag to the countless mesh compartments and laptop sleeve, this bag makes it easy to get organized, but it’s the backpack’s support features that are impressive. The Osprey bag is made with an adjustable sternum strap that comes with a whistle (particularly useful for adventurers), and a hip belt that helps evenly distribute the bag’s weight to remove strain on your back and shoulders.

It’s also built with Osprey’s proprietary suspension system, which is designed to keep the backpack close to your body, while spreading its load across the entire back panel. This prevents the bag’s weight from pulling you back, so that the bag moves with you as you travel and not against you.

Additionally, whenever you do get tired of carrying this workhorse on your back, you can use either of the convenient carrying handles on its top and left sides. Wubbenhorst also says that she particularly likes that this bag passes most airlines’ carry-on policy, even when it’s stuffed to the gills.

The Kroser Laptop Backpack is a great option for those looking for a backpack for daily use, or those who regularly go on overnight stays. It’s also a great pick for those who prefer to pack their travel backpack with just the essentials—documents, travel blanket, etc. It only holds 37 liters and features multiple pockets for easy organization, including compartments for a laptop, water bottle, pens, and valuables.

Alexandra Dubakova, head of marketing at FreeTour.com, a website for booking local tours, personally likes the Kroser backpack because it’s designed with an aqua blue lining that’s more unique than the usual basic black or brown, and, in her opinion, a lot more functional, too. “That pop of color makes it so much easier to spot my stuff inside the bag,” she says. “I’m always digging around to find things, so having that brighter interior is super handy.” It also features a collapsible steel frame box opening that can give you a bird’s-eye view of what’s in the bag.

On the downside, she doesn’t love that the zippers are bulky and visible, since they make the pockets an easy target for thieves. The bag allows you to secure the two handles with a button once it’s zippered up, though, adding an extra level of security that can provide some extra reassurance that your items will remain safe.

If you’re looking for a backpack that easily fits under your airplane seat, Away’s Active Backpack is a solid choice. It’s also a great option for those that will be taking a backpack on their daily excursions once they arrive at their destination.

First, style-wise the bag is very chic. It’s designed for the outdoors, but it’s stylish enough to take along on your metro adventures without fear it’ll clash with your outfit and be an eyesore in photos. It’s also made from abrasion-resistant material that holds up against the elements—so there’s no problem using this one in rain or snow. While on a trip to Boston, I used this bag during an unexpected flurry of snow, and the bag quickly dried without incident to any of my belongings.

I also found it to be super lightweight. I wore it on the subway to go catch my bus to Boston from New York, and I had no complaints. I packed it with quite a few things—my laptop, some full-sized beauty products, and two days of outfits—and I never felt that the bag was so heavy that I had to put it down to rest.

The Away bag features several compartments, but my favorite is the hidden pocket in the back behind the bag’s straps. I tend to get paranoid that my wallet will get swiped, but storing it back there reassured me that even the savviest thief would likely not find it. Its thoughtful design and lightweight feel make it the perfect everyday bag for longer trips, but it can be used on its own for overnight stays or weekend trips.

What stands out about this Calpak backpack is that it doubles as a duffel bag if you get tired of carrying it on your back. The backpack comes with a removable longer strap you can use to hold the duffel on a shoulder. It also comes with carrying handles—one on the top and one on the side—so you can hold it in whatever orientation feels most comfortable.

Like most of the other backpacks in this guide, it features a ton of pockets, including a zippered water bottle pocket on its left side, a laptop sleeve, an internal mesh pocket, two small zippered compartments in the front of the bag, among several others strategically placed inside the bag. The Terra is designed with a clamshell opening, which makes it relatively easy to pack, and two compression straps in the main compartment designed to hold your contents in place.

Because of its 26-liter capacity, on its own, it’s spacious enough for three to four days of travel, but keep in mind that when it’s packed to the brim, we did find that it was a little tough to get its trolley sleeve over a luggage handle. An extra feature we would have loved to see is a waist strap. It does come with a sternum strap, but a waist strap would make this backpack even more comfortable to carry. This bag does get bonus points for being made from 27 recycled plastic bottles. Sustainability is always a plus.

When shopping for a travel backpack, most brands describe how much it can hold in liters. For longer trips over three days, a larger capacity bag—40 liters or more—is the better choice.

For shorter, weekend-long excursions, you can go for backpacks with a 25- to 30-liter capacity. If you’re using your travel backpack in addition to roller luggage, or as a day bag during your travels, you can go even smaller with 18 to 22 liters. “[Too much] weight can be a bit uncomfortable when traveling around Europe and other places, so I try to stay below 40 liters,” says Tamia Ramsey, a travel expert who has been to over 40 countries and founder of the budget travel blog, Poor in a Private Plane.

Ramsey recommends backpacks with four or more compartments, since they make it easy to organize clothing, electronics, valuables, and other travel essentials. She also finds a pocket in the front particularly useful for storing things you may need on the fly, like a pen.

Other features that can make a travel backpack even more functional are a water bottle holder, a shoe or dirty laundry compartment, and a pocket that isn’t too visible for protecting your wallet, passport, and other valuables from thieves.

Finally, if you want to offset the weight of your bag, you can seek options with sternum and chest straps. These straps typically buckle along the front of your body to redistribute the backpack’s weight and take some of the strain off your back and shoulders.

Travel backpacks can open in a ton of different ways, but some of the most popular options include clamshell, U-shaped, and top-loading designs. A clamshell design opens up like a book, similar to a suitcase. These are easy to pack because they give you a bird’s-eye view of what’s in the bag. Others are top-loading, which means they open up at the top, allowing you to pack from the bottom of the bag up. Finally, backpacks with U-shaped openings are made with a zipper that goes around three sides of the backpack. These provide a wider opening than top-loading backpacks. That allows you to access things quickly without having to lay your bag down on the ground, like you would a backpack with a clamshell opening.

The type of opening you choose depends on your preference, but ones with a clamshell opening are typically the easiest to organize and pack because you get such a great view of what’s inside.

When we first wrote this story in 2025, I considered my own travel experiences, feedback from other Women’s Health editors who travel for work and personal reasons, and spoke with three travel experts who roam the globe on a regular basis. Their intel helped me evaluate the following travel backpacks for women on packability, carry comfort, design, durability, and accessibility.

#4962. Morning Minute: What Anthropic’s $1.4 Trillion IPO Means for Crypto

Morning Minute: What Anthropic’s $1.4 Trillion IPO Means for Crypto

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

💵 What Anthropic’s $1.4 Trillion IPO Means for Crypto

Several key new details for Anthropic’s pending IPO were released on Tuesday. The IPO price range was set at $70 to $78 a share, selling 825 million Class A shares to raise about $64.4 billion (at the top of the range). Pricing is expected November 18, with trading starting November 19 on Nasdaq under ANT. Roughly 20% of the deal is set aside for retail, which is about double what a book this size usually gives individual investors.

Notably on Hyperliquid, ANT is trading at $1.8T with $100M in daily trading volume and $200M in open interest (a big enough market to provide some signal). So perhaps traders are a bit more bullish on ANT price action than Wall Street.

Alongside the IPO sits a $26 billion private placement of non-voting Class N stock, bought at the IPO price by seven of Anthropic’s own compute suppliers. Google and Nvidia take about $10 billion each, AMD $2.5 billion, Broadcom, Micron and Microsoft about $1 billion apiece, TSMC $500 million. Those shares carry no lock-up. Combined with the IPO, Anthropic walks away with roughly $84.6 billion for general corporate purposes, mostly compute.

Regarding financials, Anthropic just reported revenue of $33.2 billion through nine months against $2.5 billion a year earlier and a $1.26 billion operating loss. It reported a $104 billion net loss, almost all of it a non-cash charge on convertible notes. And $900.9 billion in committed spending, with $70.7 billion due inside twelve months.

The liquidity drain thesis has been circulating since April, when CoinDesk laid out the case that mega-IPOs pull speculative money from the same pool that funds crypto. Retail allocations are the mechanism. A dollar earmarked for an IPO ticket is a dollar not in Bitcoin or memecoins.

We already ran this experiment. SpaceX listed June 12 at more than $2 trillion. Bitcoin fell 29% going into it, bottomed at $58,500 on June 30, and then rose 37% off that low. But notably, the Bitcoin bottom was effectively in on June 5 at $59k, a full week ahead of the SPCX IPO.

So the question is not whether an IPO pulls money out. It’s whether that already happened. Bitcoin fell roughly 15% from its early-October high through a $6.3 billion exchange hack, an AI capex selloff and a Democratic sweep.

Two things make this deal smaller than it looks for crypto. The retail slice is roughly $12.9 billion against SpaceX’s $22 billion, about 59% as much. And the $26 billion private placement never touches public market liquidity at all, because it comes from Anthropic’s vendors, who then get paid back in compute contracts.

Thus, if the SpaceX pattern repeats, and with the ~60% factor, we could expect BTC to drop another 5% or so into the Anthropic IPO (notably, that’s right where the 200-week moving average is). And we would expect the effective bottom to be in sometime in the next 1-2 days.

The good news is, the pain is mostly behind us, in that scenario. We will find out soon enough if IPO history repeats itself for crypto…

CoreWeave shares jumped 15.27% in premarket trading Wednesday after the AI cloud provider reported third-quarter revenue that beat analyst estimates.

#4955. 15 Chic and Surprising Christmas Tree Decorating Ideas

Nothing says the holidays have arrived like a glowing pine strung with tinsel. If you’re looking for fresh inspiration, this collection of designer-approved Christmas tree decorating ideas has it all. From playful color palettes to chic lighting ideas and nostalgic personalization, designers are sharing their best tips for decorating full-sized, pencil, and even tabletop trees. Like a freshly pruned fir, their ideas are lush and abundant: oversized bows, classic red ornaments, blue Christmas trees, and velvet ribbon turned into handmade ornaments are just a few of their favorite festive touches. Whether you prefer a real tree, artificial Christmas tree, or even a flocked Christmas tree, lean minimalist or fully embrace maximalist holiday decor, we’ve rounded up our favorite Christmas decorating ideas to help you deck your halls in style.

To personalize your tree, take inspiration from Chanel Sansregret of the Charleston-based Studio Miles Design who purchases frame ornaments with the year to fill with photos of her children every holiday season. “For me, there’s something especially moving about hanging those memories on the branches and seeing so many stages of their childhood together in one place,” she shares. “As a designer, I love a beautifully considered tree, but the sentimental details are what give it soul,” Sansregret states.

“There’s something so special about turning a corner and finding another beautifully decorated tree,” says Emily Hewett of A Well Dressed Home based in Dallas and Kansas City. In her son’s bedroom, she opted for a slender pencil tree given the room’s space constraints. “My son’s tree sits directly in front of his bedroom window, which faces the street; one of my favorite details is seeing it all lit up from the front of our home at night,” Hewett says. A pencil tree has a small footprint, yet Hewett notes it brings a little extra Christmas magic to both the inside and outside of the home.

To make a Christmas tree feel like an integral part of a room, take design cues from the room itself. Here, Hewett adorned her tree with a collection of glass bauble Christmas ornaments that she’s curated over the years. “The color palette features varying shades of blue, green, and pink, along with clear iridescent ornaments that almost resemble delicate bath bubbles,” she says. The Christmas theme was inspired by her living room palette. “Every color was intentionally pulled from the wallpapers and fabrics in my living room, creating a Christmas tree that feels like a natural extension of the space,” Hewett says.

When decorating your Christmas tree in a room with dark walls, Renee DiSanto of the Chicago area–based Park & Oak recommends using lots of twinkle lights and weaving them into the tree in a zigzag pattern. “I tend to opt for a warm white light rather than a cool blue tone for a more cozy, inviting look,” she shares. Here, the designer used bright colored decorations, a bead garland, a simple tree skirt—and plenty of twinkle lights. “A well-lit tree will always pop against dark walls,” DiSanto says.

“Bows are such a classic, but including them on a tree in a dramatic scale is what makes something traditional feel unexpected and even a bit whimsical,” says Amy Studebaker of Amy Studebaker Design. She approaches Christmas tree decorating the same way she approaches designing a home: ”With a fresh take on traditional, layers and layers of charm, and a little sprinkle of the unexpected.” For a fun and festive look, replace a traditional tree topper with a large bow and adorn branches with smaller, coordinating bows.

Brown has become the neutral this season, adding cozy sophistication to interiors and now, to holiday decor. Anastasia Casey of The Interior Collective loves the color for its warmth and versatility, and used it as the primary color on this tree. “I layered matte and metallic brown ornaments with neutrals—think muddy hues of bone, chocolate, and champagne—to create depth and avoid a flat look,” Casey shares. While the metallics catch light, the browns ground the palette, resulting in a tree that Casey says is both elevated and inviting.

To add charm and a touch of whimsy to a Christmas tree, Anastasia Casey of The Interior Collective looks for ornaments that feel collected and tell a story, whether they’re vintage finds, handmade pieces, or unique shapes. “In order to make it feel cohesive, I recommend mixing textures and scales, like pairing glass baubles with fabric or wood. It brings a sense of personality and keeps the tree from feeling too uniform,” she shares. Casey also loves incorporating dynamic elements like ribbon or bells to add movement and playfulness.

What is the correct order to decorate a Christmas tree?

The first thing in Griffin’s step-by-step approach is adding twinkle lights to the tree, then tucking in larger ornaments. “Next, I add the smaller decorative ornaments, and finally, I add the tinsel or bows on the edges of the tree limbs,” Griffin shares. Studebaker says you should have fun with decorating and not take the process too seriously; “It can still be sophisticated, but it should absolutely be a happy and playful process along the way,” the designer says.

What are the most popular Christmas tree decorations?

Designers say German glass ornaments, Christopher Radko ornaments, cascading ribbon, festive bows, and warm white lights are among the most popular Christmas tree decorations. Metallic decorations, whether gold, silver, or copper, are another favorite. “I love decorating with metallic ornaments because they reflect the light so beautifully,” Bonney-James shares.

For Bonney-James, a timeless Christmas theme includes a fresh tree. “I’ll trade messy needles for an aromatic, nostalgic experience any day,” she says. Another must-have are warm white lights, which is why she recommends checking the temperature of LED lights to make sure they don’t appear blue. The addition of silver, gold, and white decorations completes the timeless look and adds festive shine.

#4951. Bitcoin Covenants Part 5: MATT and OP_CHECKCONTRACTVERIFY — How Bitcoin could carry state across transactions

OP_CCV embeds contract state directly into Taproot output keys and enforces valid state transitions onchain. This makes it possible to achieve stateful contracts and compact fraud proofs.

This is Part 5 in the technical article series about Bitcoin covenants by Cointelegraph Research. To read the previous article click here.

MATT (Merkleize All The Things) is a smart contract framework for Bitcoin that enables stateful contracts and scalable fraud proofs. A stateful contract carries information forward across multiple transactions and can update this information as the contract evolves. Standard Bitcoin scripts do not provide this capability, because each transaction is evaluated independently with no memory of previous executions.

Every covenant proposal allows state-carrying UTXOs to an extent, as illustrated by scripting languages such as Sapio, which builds on OP_CTV. This works by using addresses to represent a finite state automaton, where transactions function as the state transitions. However, there is no way to elegantly implement numerical logic in this paradigm, a gap filled by MATT, and in particular its proposed implementation through OP_CCV. Because MATT allows committed data to be computed at spend time and re-embedded into the next output, each spend can derive a new state dynamically, instead of choosing from a predetermined set of states.

To understand the MATT proposal, it is useful to first review how Taproot commits data through public keys. Every Taproot output contains a base public key, called the internal key, which can be cryptographically modified or “tweaked” to commit to extra information. Formally, the tweaked public key is Q = P + t·G, where P is the internal key, G is the generator of the secp256k1 elliptic curve, and t is a tagged hash of P and the committed data.

In a regular Taproot output, this commitment typically encodes the Merkle root of a taptree, which contains hidden spending paths. MATT builds on this mechanism to also embed contract state into the key. Instead of embedding complex logic directly in Bitcoin’s scripting language, MATT encodes computation as a sequence of state commitments, and the chain verifies only that each state transition is valid.

MATT can be realized through different opcode combinations. The most direct and well-specified implementation relies on OP_CHECKCONTRACTVERIFY (OP_CCV). OP_CCV provides the primitive that makes state-carrying UTXOs feasible within Taproot.  OP_CCV allows a script to verify that a Taproot input or output key has been tweaked to commit to a specific piece of state data. The internal key is first tweaked with a hash of the contract’s current state, and then Taproot’s standard taptree tweak is applied on top. The resulting output key therefore commits to both the tapscript  (the set of spending conditions) and the current state of the contract.

OP_CCV can then enforce what state is carried forward into the next output. It allows the script to inspect the state data provided on the witness stack, perform computations on it, and require that the resulting updated state is correctly re-committed in a specified output. This is how state transitions are enforced on-chain. The stack format is:

OP_CHECKCONTRACTVERIFY

The opcode supports three modes for checking an output as well as a separate input mode. In default mode, the entire BTC value of the input UTXO is allocated to the specified output; in deduct mode, only a portion equal to the output’s amount is assigned, leaving the remainder available for further outputs; and in ignore mode, the script and data commitment are verified without constraining the amount.  Because default and deduct modes are designed to preserve value across checked outputs, transaction fees must be paid through separate fee-paying inputs. A dedicated fee input and change output can be added to the transaction without conflicting with the covenant’s constraints. This prevents the accidental burning of funds that can occur in more primitive covenant implementations, such as OP_CTV or SIGHASH_ANYPREVOUT. It also addresses a major limitation of presigned-transaction-style covenants, which typically define amounts upfront and therefore cannot accommodate dynamic fee adjustments or partial unvaults.

When OP_CCV is combined with OP_CAT, the full MATT framework becomes even more expressive. OP_CAT enables byte concatenation in Script, which makes it possible to construct and verify Merkle proofs directly on-chain. Two distinct Merkle trees are associated with a Taproot output in such an arrangement. The first is the familiar Taproot script tree (the taptree), whose leaves are the contract’s spending scripts. The second is a separate state tree, whose leaves are the contract’s data, such as the intermediate steps of a computation.  The OP_CCV + OP_CAT script can verify a Merkle proof against the current state tree root, compute the updated root after a transition, and use OP_CCV to force the next output to commit to it.

This construction enables fraud-proof systems with a slashing condition in which a long computation is committed off-chain, and only the specific disputed step (with its path in the binary tree) needs to be revealed and verified on-chain. Similar systems exist as optimistic rollups on other blockchain systems: the full computation runs off-chain, and the on-chain contract only intervenes when someone raises a challenge. Fraud proofs require that the computation be compiled down to a binary circuit or equivalent step-by-step representation that can be committed to in a Merkle tree. OP_CCV + OP_CAT would avoid the need for the Lamport one-time signatures and pre-signed transaction graphs that BitVM relies on to enforce state consistency.

Beyond fraud proofs, the OP_CCV and OP_CAT combination also has implications for vaults. OP_CCV alone can implement vaults, but when combined with OP_CAT, it allows fully featured vaults that are essentially equivalent to the functionality proposed by OP_VAULT. As James O’Beirne, the author of OP_VAULT, has noted, OP_CCV represents a more general and robust design that effectively supersedes his earlier proposal.

In the next article in this series, we will move on from general covenant proposals to review application-specific opcodes such as OP_VAULT, OP_UNVAULT, and OP_EVICT. Each of the opcodes are designed to solve a specific problem rather than provide broad programmability.

#4950. Fashion Maverick Christopher John Rogers Unveils Bold New Carpets for The Rug Company

For Christopher John Rogers, the magic of a garment lies in its making. “I was thinking about preserving what happens in the studio—draping, cutting, chalk lines, thread—and the mistakes, smears, and imperfections that are left by the hand,” notes the Louisiana-born, New York–based fashion designer. That ethos became the departure point for his new collection, The Studio by Christopher John Rogers for The Rug Company. Conceived in partnership with the brand’s creative director, Will Cooper, the line builds upon The Rug Company’s history of collaborations with fashion greats such as Alexander McQueen, Diane von Furstenberg, and Vivienne Westwood.

Marker Test rug showcased in Will Cooper's apartment

Another rug from The Studio By Christopher John Rogers for The Rug Company collection in Cooper's living area

Spanning a range of flatweaves and plush piles, crafted using hand-knotted and woven techniques, Rogers’s six designs reference the abstract paintings of Morris Louis and Ellsworth Kelly in addition to his own archival runway looks and creative process. “The patterns came from something as simple as my marker tests on paper,” he says, recalling how he reinterpreted those scribbles, which date to his 2022 pre-fall runway collection, in silk and wool. “I’ve always loved the idea that something accidental can become the very thing you want to preserve.”

Whereas Paintstroke evokes a wet stripe of pigment, Chalk Line juxtaposes raised cross-hatchings against a neutral ground. Mixing faded primary colors with warm earth tones, the palette channels Rogers’s signature hues through a subdued lens, the construction of each piece adding a sense of patina. “We wanted the rugs to feel like they could be from any decade; they could be vintage pieces,” says Cooper, who was introduced to Rogers by past brand collaborator Farrow & Ball. “They reflect Christopher’s evolution and traces of the many hands involved in their making—histories that ultimately become part of the home.”

#4946. In This Midcentury Nashville Home a Mosaic Rainbow-Hued Window Brings Color to the Earthy Interiors

Here’s one you may not have heard before: What do Serge Mouille sconces, a Louis Poulsen artichoke pendant, and Borge Mogensen dining chairs have in common? Hint, hint: They all live in the Nashville home of Doug and Carina Jenkins, the founders of antiques and vintage furniture store Eneby Home.

One might reasonably presume the home of antiques purveyors to be chock-full of one-of-a-kind finds from far-flung locales, and while that is certainly the case here, the husband-and-wife duo’s house otherwise expertly subverts expectations.

Built in 1970, the uber-quiet four-acre property sits at the top of a hill overlooking seemingly endless rolling valleys. It’s surrounded by native honeysuckle bushes and Tennessee Fieldstone, and the sun sets on the front side of the home at 6:37 p.m., says Doug, who dreamed of building what he describes as an “art gallery” for their furniture.

The Jenkinses kept the wall of original Tennessee Fieldstone—and the original copper fireplace—but added their own flair with vintage furnishings, like a La Mura sofa, Gianfranco Frattini for Cassina leather lounge chairs, a Charles Dudouyt credenza, and a 1960s marble fossil coffee table from Italy. “It’s super unique,” says Doug. “It doesn’t have a name behind it.”

Doug and Carina Jenkins, the founders of Eneby Home, with their daughters. They kept the original Tennessee Fieldstone on the wall and paired it with an antique marble dining table that’s “huge and heavy,” says Doug. “The guys hated bringing stuff to our house.” Even though the Louis Poulsen artichoke pendant is often seen in copper, the Jenkins were able to source one with a green finish, seen here. The chairs are Borge Mogensen; the 1960s geometric painting is from France.

A well-lit workspace features a vintage 1950s desk, an Italian cocoon lamp, and a Hans Wegner chair from Denmark.

“There’s not a lot of this type of house in Nashville,” says Greg Dutton, the founder and architect behind Columbus, Ohio-based and AD PRO Directory design firm Dutton Studio. “But we had to look past what was there—a mixed bag with a new era in each room—to create a backdrop for the work they do.”

Cue the blank slate. The couple bought the house as-is—black toilets, mustard-hued siding, and early 2000s kitchen included—and embarked on a “bigger than expected” renovation, says Doug, that translated to moving walls, updating floor plans, a new roof and flooring, and overall furnishings, of course.

With the resulting minimalist white walls and clean lines, how does one amass the amount of character evident here? “When you build in materials that have inherent imperfection, that naturally bakes in a lot of warmth,” says Dutton. Think locally sourced, character-grade white oak flooring throughout; imported reclaimed European bluestone that’s well over 100 years old in the primary bath; and an original copper fireplace in the living room.

“The mosaic supposedly came from a building in Bologna,” says Doug. “The morning sun comes up on this side” and ignites a fire of filtered light within the home.

“The island has a commercial look to it,” says Doug of the Vipp system. “It hit every box. It’s sick.” Dutton helped reconfigure the space’s floor plan to open the room to the dining area. The oven is by Miele.

A Guillerme et Chambron cabinet, sourced from Eneby Home, houses curiosities. The 1960s pendant light is Stilnovo.

The guest bathroom originally had a black toilet and smoked glass. Now it features vintage Serge Mouille sconces, of which Doug has “never seen another pair,” a 250-year-old antique Italian mirror from the 1800s, and a Josef Hoffman bench.

“Flaws make the house feel better, not worse,” says Dutton. “But creating something that looks effortless actually takes a lot of coordination. We wanted it to feel like it had just happened that way, and installing materials that feel like they’ve been there forever automatically gives the house permanence and ensures it’ll age gracefully over time.”

The push-and-pull between old and new, simple and full of character, clean and distressed is one the Jenkins family is familiar with. The balancing act makes the house interesting, says Doug, and the same inclination to lean on historic materials translates just as well to furnishings.

“Some of this stuff we’d been saving in hopes we’d one day be able to use it for our own house,” says Doug. One such example is a 1940s or ’50s-era, rainbow-hued mosaic window the couple bought five years ago and stored in a crate ever since. “We asked Greg to change the layout of the wall to make it work,” says Doug. The stone sculpture on display in front of the window is another; it’s 2,000 years old, from Rome.

An earthy palette in the primary bedroom lets the landscape through the windows do the talking. The sconces are by Biny; the oversized 1880 landscape painting is from France; the chairs are Borge Mogensen; the side table is Charles Dudouyt, and the floor lamp is La Borne.

“The fireplace is original, and we ran with it,” says Doug. “It’s New Mexico style and is super cool.” It’s paired with an abstract geometric art piece and a collection of vases from Denmark.

The stainless steel Vipp island in the kitchen, which the couple found in Denmark, is “the best thing in the house,” says Carina. “We moved from a house with a big pantry, and it was a challenge to figure out where everything would go. This island holds a lot.” Here, the family embraces the “European model” of compact living–albeit in 4,500 square feet. Dutton designed the black cabinetry wall as a backdrop for the island, which he calls a “piece of jewelry.”

Like any gallerist worth his salt, Doug spends five minutes each night turning off all of the ambient lighting in the house, like a pair of midcentury Biny sconces in the primary bedroom, an Orocolo lamp in the living room, and a 1970s Marika Carniti Bollea Nautilus lamp in the kitchen. Much of the light shines on a collection of artwork that’s so vast it’s often swapped out.

“We’ve already traded a few things,” says Doug. “If we find something we love, we want to use it. It’s what we do. It’s a perk, for sure.”

As is a home that’s forever. “Our girls want to come here when we’re old with their own kids,” says Carina. “They love it so much.”

The 1950s painting is from Denmark, the 1970s vase is from Sweden, the 1970s stool is Pierre Chapo, and the ceramic is from France. “We spend a lot of time in Denmark and like to mix Danish and Swedish,” says Doug. “We know a lot about that style of furniture.”

Dutton reworked the floor plan in the primary bathroom to set the vanity under a skylight. “We don’t use much overhead lighting,” says Carina.
