#4942. Paraguay bank collapse shows stablecoins can offer a lifeline... of sorts

Ueno’s collapse exposed the fragility of digital banking in Paraguay, as customers scrambled for access to their money and stablecoins offered a lifeline.

The collapse of Paraguay’s digital lender Ueno Bank last week left nearly three million customers — roughly 60% of Paraguay’s adults — unable to access their accounts.

Paraguay’s Central Bank placed Ueno into resolution on Nov. 3 after its solvency ratio fell below half the legally required minimum. An investigation uncovered questionable lending practices and deficiencies in credit documentation and accounting.

The collapse also triggered a political crisis, with thousands taking to the streets to protest and President Santiago Peña attempting to resign before being impeached and arrested on charges of breach of trust and using his office for personal gain.

Paraguay’s peer-to-peer crypto market immediately reflected the turmoil, with some sellers asking for a USDt premium of up to 13%. “USDt is a lifeline for hundreds millions of people in the world,” Tether chief executive Paolo Ardoino said.

For many, the incident sparked uncertainty over savings, wages and the money needed to cover everyday expenses. With the local guaraní (PYG) currency falling 12.3% in a week, some turned to stablecoins to maintain the value of their funds, or just to keep payments moving along.

David Eisenmann, founder and chief executive of Paraguayan crypto exchange X4T, tells Magazine the timing made the liquidity need even more pronounced.

“The intervention came in the first days of the month,” he says, “exactly when salaries, rent and supplier invoices are due,” adding that many business owners “couldn’t make payroll.” For some, USDt was “suddenly the most liquid money” they had:

But beyond another TradFi masterclass in the virtues of self-custody, Ueno’s collapse highlights the growing role of dollar-pegged stablecoins providing an alternative source of liquidity when traditional banks fail.

Eisenmann says X4T processed more than $10 million in local on-ramp volume over the 30 days to early November, but some of that activity predated Ueno’s collapse.

“The dollar began recovering against the guaraní in October,” he says, “and the Ueno news sped up a move that was already underway.”

Related: Stablecoins can drain from banks and nations at lightning speed

He says the activity “runs in both directions,” with some customers selling crypto for guaraníes or dollars to cover payments they could no longer make through Ueno, while “demand for USDt and USDC as a dollar store of value has risen sharply.”

Paraguay’s Central Bank placed Ueno into resolution on Nov. 3. Source: Central Bank of Paraguay

As of Nov. 9 the Central Bank said 93.1% of customers with Ueno deposits had been repaid. Despite this confidence has been shaken and X4T continued to see higher online inquiries for dollar-denominated stablecoins. In fact, Eisenmann says the level “has not changed significantly” from the week of the collapse.

Rhys Hicks, associate analyst at data and analytics company GlobalData, says the digital bank’s failure has shown “how much can go wrong when governance does not keep pace with growth.”

That said, he’s not seeing evidence of a mass migration from Paraguayan banks into stablecoins, since other banks “picked up the slack,” and people “seem to have been angrier” with the government than with the banks.

“Some consumers will look for different ways to keep their money safe, but I do not see an overwhelming shift to stablecoins yet,” he says.

Seeking dollars when confidence in domestic banks falters is hardly new. In Argentina’s 2001 financial crisis, the government imposed the “corralito,” restricting cash withdrawals from bank accounts, following a run on deposits.

Related: Peter Thiel backs crypto-friendly Silicon Valley Bank rival — Report

Amid a deepening crisis over the country’s currency peg and sovereign debt, the government later converted dollar-denominated deposits into pesos, reducing people’s savings by about 30% overnight and further damaging confidence in the country’s fraying banking system.

Tether CEO calls USDt a lifeline. Source: Paolo Ardoino

But stablecoins aren’t decentralized and they are not insulated from the banking system either.

During the March 2023 collapse of Silicon Valley Bank, stablecoin issuer Circle disclosed that $3.3 billion of USDC reserves, around 8% of the total, were held at the lender. USDC briefly depegged and fell to 86 cents on secondary markets after Circle said it could not withdraw the funds.

A BIS paper on stablecoins and the international monetary system warns that widespread use of dollar-backed tokens could create challenges for monetary sovereignty in emerging markets.

Hicks says stablecoins can provide an alternative during periods of political instability and “low trust in the local system” because they are pegged to a more stable currency, like the US dollar.

But regulators need to address “transparency” and “consumer protection,” he says, because stablecoin holders don’t benefit from the same deposit guarantees as bank customers, and there is no central bank to step in and make them whole.

Eisenmann says he expects the incident to spark more “diversification,” with people “spreading their money across banks, cash and digital assets” instead of relying on a single institution.

“How quickly trust in the banks returns will depend on how quickly and transparently the authorities handle the resolution,” he says.

#4941. Victorian Architecture Meets Mediterranean Charm in This Warm and Whimsical London Home

Interior designer Marta de la Rica describes herself as “a New Yorker by birth, French by adoption, and Spanish at heart.” That mix of cultures would prove to be an asset for one of her recent projects: the 5,382-square-foot four-story home of a family of four in London. A fine example of Victorian architecture, the building, located in the quiet residential neighborhood of Battersea and only steps away from Battersea Park, was built in 1879. “I already knew the clients, since I had previously designed their home in Bilbao, and I knew what kind of atmosphere the family wanted as well as what was particularly important to them about their home,” says de la Rica.

The walls of the stairwell are painted in Yel-01 Smooth by Clayworks and the Clam Shell Large light fixtures are by Porta Romana. The floor is made of several different marbles: Negro Marquina, Perlado, and Rojo Alicante.

The young family is originally from Spain but had already been living in London for nearly 10 years when they decided to buy a single-family home there. De la Rica decided to design spaces for them with a British-Mediterranean look that reflects the character of the historic Victorian architecture and the demands of modern family life. “The family wanted an elegant and peaceful home that, above all, would be warm and cozy,” says de la Rica. “For us, that meant creating spaces with comfort, character, and personality.”

Two midcentury armchairs sit in front of a Louis XIV fireplace from 1700.

Light brown and rust tones dominate the living room’s color palette.

Since the clients are Spanish, the project had a particularly interesting cultural dimension for the designer, as she explains: “We wanted the house to feel personal to them while also remaining true to its London identity and the Victorian architecture. So the project became a dialogue between English interior design and a more relaxed, Mediterranean lifestyle,” says de la Rica.

De la Rica’s interiors combine English interior design and a relaxed lifestyle in this Victorian building where the sunroom doubles as a dining room.

Originally, the family had been looking for a home in a different part of London, but when they saw this house, they were immediately taken with it and changed their plans. The building’s structure was in very good condition; only the floor plan needed to be reconfigured. “Above all, we wanted to preserve the spacious rooms, the ceiling heights, the bay windows, and the staircase,” explains the designer.

A relaxed breakfast setting with chairs by Vintola Studio and Benjamin pendant lights from Felix Lighting,

Visually, all the rooms have an almost opulent feel, yet at the same time, each one reflects its particular purpose. The ground floor includes a study, a living room, a kitchen, af amily room, and a sunroom, which also serves as the dining room. The parents’ bedroom is located on the second floor. A separate children’s area with bedrooms and play areas was created on the top floor. “The sunroom is a big part of the character of the house. To build the basement, it had to be demolished and rebuilt. It was very important to us to preserve the extraordinary presence of this space and its connection to the rest of the house,” says de la Rica.

The designer made the transitions from one room to the next as natural as possible by ensuring that the materials and atmosphere flow seamlessly.

The living spaces flow into the garden (designed by Matthew Wilson) and the terraces in front of the family room and the sunroom provide direct access to the outdoors. The garden features a pool, an outdoor kitchen, and a spacious dining area, providing additional spaces for social gatherings. The interior is characterized by elegant craftsmanship, custom details, warm colors, and high-quality materials. These choices highlight the grandeur of the historic home while also creating a cozy atmosphere.

A 20th-century dresser serves as a nightstand in the primary bedroom; an onyx lamp sits next to a vase filled with flowers.

“I particularly like that the rooms all have their own character, yet there is a clear, unifying design that carries throughout the house,” says the designer. It was also important to de la Rica to emphasize the scale of the house without having it feel cold or ostentatious. “Even in the larger rooms, there’s a coziness. It was very important to us to achieve this balance,” she says.

In the guest bathroom, the walls are painted in Yel-01 Smooth by Clayworks. Below the Negro Marquina marble sink by MDLR-Design, a curtain of Marly fabric by Colony.

The playroom showcases the Victorian architecture.

The children’s rooms are located under the mansard roof, which is typical of many Victorian houses in London. For the two youngest members of the family, the designer transformed the entire top floor with its sloped ceilings into colorful playrooms.

Amid dinosaurs and Mickey Mouse toys, the family’s son sleeps here. A Globe ceiling light hangs above the bed (Olofane).

De la Rica’s favorite parts of the house, however, are the living room and the dining room. For her, these are the areas where the home truly comes to life and where the design directly interacts with the family’s daily routine.

Above the large sofa in the family room, a Soren globe light from Pinch. The cream-colored Mayan Cuzco rug is from Bellbridge.

She particularly likes that the living room, dining room, and kitchen form a single, open space. “This part of the house allows for a very uncomplicated way of living,” she explains. “It’s very beautiful but not contrived or overly formal. It exudes coziness and spontaneity, and for me, that’s ultimately what makes a family home.”

For the walls in this bathroom, the designer chose Oli-03 Smooth by Clayworks, wall sconces by Vaughan, and a Calacatta Viola marble sink.

The wardrobe in the guest bedroom is upholstered with Ticking Stripe 1 Black fabric (Ian Mankin). The decorative Flax 03 tassels are from Samuel & Sons.

In the daughter’s bedroom, the rug is from Kvadrat. The closet doors are upholstered with fabrics from Yutes.

The Bert & May tiles in Garnet and Sand White were custom-made for this bathroom. The bathtub is Perlado marble.

#4937. Inside the Underground Mosques of Kazakhstan’s Surreal Mangystau Desert

Up until about 20 years ago, a faint trail up a steep hill through limestone-and-chalk cliffs was the only way to reach Beket-Ata, one of the sacred underground mosques—as they’re colloquially known—of Kazakhstan’s Mangystau desert. “There was no proper path then, but people made the difficult climb in the heat and wind,” my guide, Yerzhan Nagmanov, tells me as we embark on our way to the holy site—eased slightly by the stairs now carved into the stone. That arduous journey, the travelers believed, “was part of the pilgrimage.”

En route to Beket-Ata with Mangystau Tour 999, visitors walk a trail cutting through the steep hill.

The trail of stairs descending into the canyon en route to Beket-Ata.

As inhospitable as the roughly 64,000-square-mile desert may seem, it has attracted scores of people over the years arriving to see Beket-Ata and the four other sacred complexes scattered across its expanse: Shopan-Ata, Karaman-Ata, Shakpak-Ata and Sultan-Epe. Traveling between the five holy sites, the distinction between architecture and geology evaporates into the desert heat: Walls, ceilings, and floors are all variations of the rock from which the mosques were excavated. For pilgrims, the journey to these sacred sites is an act of devotion, where people come to honor revered Sufi masters, pray for blessings, and seek spiritual guidance in times of difficulty.

The mosques are hundreds of years old, collectively dating between the 12th and 18th centuries, but they’re new to the UNESCO World Heritage List as of this summer. The “Rock Mosques and Associated Sacred Sites of Mangystau” were added to the esteemed collection of landmarks in July.

Each mosque is associated with a different spiritual master within Sufism, the mystical branch of Islam. The five Sufi masters’ influence shaped the region’s religious landscape, and then the landscape itself, as all of the mosques—crafted in a rock-cut style that grew out of nomadic building traditions—seem to grow directly from the desert terrain; they’re carved into caves, tucked along the contours of cliffs and hillsides, and tunneled below the ground.

After three weeks travelling through Kazakhstan’s cities, mountains, and pine forests, I spent my final week in Mangystau, a landscape so different from anything I’d seen so far in the country that it felt like a whole world unto itself. Having recently visited Astana’s Grand Mosque, with its blue domes, soaring minarets, and gleaming expanses of marble, I wondered whether Mangystau’s rock-cut shrines might fall flat by comparison. For me, however, their lack of splashy grandeur is precisely what made them so compelling.

A stately arch welcomes travelers to the steps leading to Beket-Ata.

While I am not Muslim, I was fascinated by how the Sufi masters traversed this unforgiving terrain along with their families. Some walked hundreds of kilometers to establish places of worship far from the comforts of the world, spending years in prayer and contemplation before eventually being buried here. I was drawn to the way the rock mosques embody a humble yet stunning expression of faith shaped by the austere landscape, where instead of gilded shrines or massive palaces, devotion found a more organic form. Through guided 4x4 tours with Red Maya Travel and Mangystau Tour 999, I visited all five.

A necropolis inside the Shopan-Ata complex.

My first stop on the tour is Shopan-Ata, named after the 12th-century Sufi teacher. The complex consists of a vast necropolis (a large burial ground with tombs of religious figures and generations of pilgrims) of more than 2,000 monuments, including domed mausoleums and kulpytas, which are ornately carved stone steles traditionally used as Kazakh gravestones. The 12-chamber mosque is carved into the rock, and a shaft of daylight is cast onto two sacred wooden poles below. According to a local tradition, pilgrims recite a prayer and circle the poles three times counterclockwise, which is believed to help the spirits hear them. Visitors also tie strips of white cloth around the poles, the knot symbolically binding a prayer to the sacred site. These practices likely come from Shamanic and Tengrian traditions of tying strips of cloth to sacred trees and mountain passes to honor nature spirits––customs that became intertwined with local forms of Muslim saint veneration over time.

Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite; these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

Domed mausoleums at the Shopan-Ata necropolis.

The entrance to Shopan-Ata, shaded by a mulberry tree. These mosques were built around oases that sustained life in the harsh desert climate.

Legend holds that centuries later, Shopan-Ata appeared to Beket-Ata in a dream, offering spiritual guidance, making this shrine an important stop before continuing to Beket-Ata. Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite, its water an incongruous presence in a landscape this dry––a reminder that these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

The sacred wooden pole, around which visitors tie white cloth to seek blessings.

The final few stairs leading up to the Beket-Ata shrine.

Just over an hour away, Beket-Ata is perhaps the best-known of the five mosques. Founded by the 18th-century Sufi teacher Beket Myrzagululy, the shrine lies in a remote canyon about 170 miles from Aktau, the Caspian-side gateway to Mangystau. The steep descent into the canyon is a reminder that the effort needed to reach these shrines has always been a part of the devotion.

The structure is carved into the pale rock, its interconnected chambers following the curves of the cliff. Narrow openings in the ceilings pull shafts of daylight into the cool, dim rooms, where rugs are spread across the stone floor.

Communal dining is integral to Kazakh culture.

An imam is usually on hand to receive visitors and share information about the site. As we settle on the carpets, he details each of the chambers, one of which contains Beket-Ata’s grave, and another is associated with his sister. The remaining rooms are used for prayer, with separate spaces for men and women.

My pilgrimage continues into the dining hall, where a long, continually replenished table runs through the room, with pilgrims sitting on either side and eating together. The mosque provides some freshly cooked meat free of charge, while visitors leave bread, sweets, tea, yogurt, jam and other provisions for those who arrive after them—an especially vivid expression of Central Asian hospitality.

Next we drive across remote country to the Tyub-Karagan Peninsula for Shakpak-Ata, whose cruciform mosque is carved into a massive chalk cliff. Its porous white surface, which looks almost like honeycomb against the desert, was shaped millions of years ago when this part of Mangystau lay beneath the ancient Tethys Sea. Inside, drawings and inscriptions in Arabic, Persian, and Turkic—some dating back to the 18th century—offer a tangible record of the pilgrims who traveled here from across Central Asia, long before air-conditioned 4x4s and Starlink internet made traversing this unforgiving landscape simpler.

A honeycomb-like pattern along the rock face of Shakpak-Ata.

Slightly west, Sultan-Epe lies beside a deep canyon, where a stream threads through thickets of mulberry, buckthorn, blackberry, and wild herbs—another improbable pocket of green in one of Kazakhstan’s driest regions. Sultan-Epe is revered as a protector of seafarers and fishermen—a fitting association for a shrine overlooking the Caspian. According to local tradition, Sultan-Epe was the son of Hakim-Ata, a renowned Sufi poet and disciple of Khoja Ahmed Yasawi, which connects the shrine to the spiritual tradition associated with Shopan-Ata and, through his later spiritual mentor, Beket-Ata.

The stacked-stone entrance to Sultan-Epe.

The final stop on the circuit is Karaman-Ata, where a seven-hectare necropolis stretches across a low, open plot of desert dotted with funerary monuments, some more than 1,000 years old. The mosque is carved into the slope of a small marl hill, and as you descend into the rock, the temperature drops noticeably from the heat outside. A narrow passage leads to a circular room believed to be associated with Sufi rituals including devotional whirling, a type of worship that is expressed through physical movement.

Karaman-Ata, the largest of the five rock mosques of Kazhakstan’s Mangystau desert.

Reaching each site still means long drives across remote terrain, with limited to no mobile coverage or fuel services–making a sturdy vehicle and a trusted local guide essential. But after days spent traveling the region, these places begin to alter your sense of the desert. Around a well, beneath a mulberry tree, or at a table crowded with food and strangers, the landscape that seemed so stark and inhospitable reveals an unexpected generosity, preserved in the rituals of those who continue to make the journey here.

#4933. 9 Best Desks for Small Spaces That Offer Savvy Organization (2026)

A desk in a small apartment rarely gets a room of its own. More often it shares a wall with the bed, borrows a corner of the living room, or has to hide somewhere when guests come over. This means the best desks for small spaces really earn their footprint. We’ve rounded up nine that do just that, including a wall desk that puts vertical space to work, a folding desk that collapses to under three inches, and several other petite options that hold their own as furniture, once the laptop is stowed.

Each pick is sized for a compact home office, a bedroom work setup, or an unclaimed nook, and we’ve noted dimensions, materials, and assembly for all of them. If you have a little more room to work with, many have bigger siblings in the same collection.

If you want a standing desk that doesn’t look like office equipment, turn to Article’s Torme. It adjusts its height with a manual lift mechanism hidden in the top, so you can switch between sitting and standing without a motor or a control panel. And while there are no drawers or additional shelves, a few divided compartments are within the desk’s body to keep your daily essentials close by.

For those interested in a work surface that can double as a vanity or living room sideboard, the Sloane Desk from Castlery is up to the task. Elevated by fluted panels and a duo of discreet storage drawers, its look is polished enough to nimbly complement non-office settings. At just over 47 inches wide, it also has room to work as a small computer desk with a monitor, and the drawers keep chargers and paperwork out of sight when it’s off duty.

This Scandi-style desk is all about taking up vertical space—one of our favorite space-saving hacks. Blending metal and wood in one streamlined design, it provides a top shelf for vertical storage and plenty of wall space to add artwork and decor. Choose between a black or white metal frame to match your home’s mood.

A treat for the eyes—and a space-saving solution for any small home—this compact wood desk pairs its clean, design-forward aesthetic with discreet, functional details: a drawer for extra storage, a hidden cubby for cable management, and a fully finished back that allows it to be displayed anywhere in the room. With solid wood legs and slats, it’s a sturdy home office addition that’s both handsome and heirloom-worthy. At $2,495, it’s the priciest pick here. But it’s also the most likely to outlast the apartment it was bought for.

This electric standing desk changes desk height at the touch of a button. At 34 inches wide, it’s the narrowest standing desk on this list. Available in three sizes and color combinations, its ice-blue finish provides a welcome break from ho-hum neutrals—and, if you’d like, you can order a desk chair in the exact same color for a sleek, monochrome setup.

The ultimate small-space-friendly desk is one that folds away when not in use. The Wohomo folding desk collapses to under three inches, so it can slide behind a door or into a closet between workdays. This Amazon option boasts thousands of positive reviews touting its slim profile, and it’s light enough for one person to move on their own. Available in three sizes (31 inches, 39 inches, and 47 inches), you’re able to choose how much workspace you’ll need. This desk is also equipped with a side hook ideal for keeping your headphones, work bag, or jacket off the ground.

When AD featured Emma Chamberlain’s Los Angeles home in 2022, she was already learning the ropes of decorating with the interiors firm Proem. She has since followed that project with a collection for West Elm. The house taught her to weigh function as heavily as looks. “If something looks great but feels bad, it doesn’t make the cut,” she previously told us. The 36-inch mini desk, shown here in dark walnut, is narrow enough for a bedroom corner or the end of a living room, and at $499 it lands in the middle of this list price-wise. If you have the wall for it, the collection also includes a 48-inch storage desk at $899.

What Are the Best Types of Desks for Small Spaces?

For most small spaces, the best options are wall-mounted desks and floating desks, which keep the floor clear and put vertical space to use. Folding desks are another great option since they’re easy to stow away when you’re done for the day. If you have an empty corner space, a corner desk or a small L-shaped desk can claim square footage that otherwise goes unused. A secretary desk, which closes over your work, makes sense if your home office is also your living room.

How Do I Choose the Right Small Computer Desk for My Needs?

The right computer desk for you will depend on your priorities. If you have multiple monitors, choose a desk with a longer shape and a cord organizer to ensure you have enough surface area. If you work on just a laptop, consider a portable C-shaped desk that can double as a side table when not in use. If your desk is too shallow for a keyboard and a screen, a keyboard tray mounted underneath buys you room without a bigger footprint.

While most sitting desks are between 27 and 30 inches tall, your height will play a factor in ergonomics and how comfortable you’ll feel sitting for long periods of time. For example, if you’re taller, you may find yourself slouching to type. For those who are shorter, your neck could be placed at an odd angle for viewing your laptop or monitor screen. If you’d rather keep the desk you already have, a standing desk converter sits on top of it and gives you an adjustable height without adding another piece of furniture.

Branch, a desk and office-chair company that is focused on ergonomics, recommends that the best desk height maintains a 90-degree angle at your elbows when your arms rest on the desk (a.k.a. while you’re typing). Your desk chair will also affect your comfort and ergonomic posture. You can shop our tested and researched picks in our office chairs guide.

What Are the Most Space-Efficient Desks for Small Apartments?

Vertical wall-mounted desks, foldable desks (basically the Murphy bed of workspaces), and C-shaped portable desks are going to be the most space-efficient options for small apartments. Foldable desks can easily be tucked away when not in use, while C-shaped tables can also function as a drinks table after working hours. If you need a space-saving desk with storage, look for vertical storage above the work surface rather than drawers below it, which keeps the desk’s footprint small.

Where Should I Put a Desk in a Small Apartment?

Start with the power outlets. A desk within reach of a wall plug saves you from running extension cords across the room, and it gives you an obvious place for a phone and laptop charging station. After that, look for natural light and a wall or corner that isn’t already doing another job.

#4932. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Barclays’ BlackRock Bitcoin ETF holdings rose 48% in the third quarter, pro-crypto lawmaker Tom Emmer was elected House Republican minority whip as Democrats prepare to take control of Congress and hotter-than-expected US inflation raised concerns about a potential half-point Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

Barclays reported holding 3.94 million shares in BlackRock’s spot Bitcoin exchange-traded fund (ETF) at the end of the third quarter, up 48% from the previous quarter.

The position in the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT) was worth about $187 million on Sept. 30, up from $88.5 million for 2.66 million shares on June 30, according to Barclays’ Friday Form 13F filing.

“Early readings suggest longer-term holders make up a growing share of the spot bitcoin ETF investor base,” Bitwise analyst Camran Khosravi told Cointelegraph, pointing to increased bank exposure, including at Citigroup.

Citigroup reported a new position worth about $119 million in the Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC). Its reported IBIT holdings also rose 50% to about 521,000 shares, from 347,000 at the end of June.

The filings offer an early indication of changing institutional Bitcoin ETF ownership, with more major banks and hedge funds yet to disclose their third-quarter positions. Institutional investment managers have until Monday to file their third-quarter holdings reports.

Pro-crypto US Representative Tom Emmer has been elected House Republican minority whip, securing the party’s second-highest leadership position as Democrats prepare to take control of Congress in January.

Emmer beat Texas Representative August Pfluger in a 103-76 secret ballot vote, according to Politico. He previously served as House Republican majority whip and will remain the party’s No. 2 leader, this time under incoming Minority Leader Jim Jordan.

As minority whip, Emmer will be responsible for rallying Republican votes, coordinating the party’s legislative strategy and helping maintain unity on key bills.

“Tom has been one of the strongest advocates for digital assets in Congress, and he has led the fight against a government-controlled CBDC and stood up for American innovators,” GOP Senator Cynthia Lummis said on X.

Crypto industry experts warned on Tuesday that higher-than-expected US inflation could lead to a another Federal Reserve interest rate hike, making borrowing more expensive and potentially discouraging investment in cryptocurrencies.

The US Consumer Price Index (CPI) rose 0.4% month over month in October, above economists’ forecast of 0.3%, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics - a slower increase than September’s 0.6% rise. Core inflation, which excludes food and energy prices, rose 0.3%, above the expected 0.2%.

Currently, the Federal Reserve holds its benchmark interest rate at 3.75% to 4.00%, after raising it by a quarter-point in September and leaving it unchanged in October.

The report came just a few hours after Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller said in London that another core inflation reading of 0.3% or higher could prompt the Fed to consider a larger interest rate hike in December.

“In that case, I believe a 50-basis-point hike in the policy rate should be considered at the December meeting,” Waller said. “Hiking only 25-basis-points would put our credibility at risk, a risk I am not eager to take.”

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy.

#4930. How to Train for a 5K, According to a Running Coach

And how to run your next one even faster.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

There's a reason runners love the 5K distance. It's not too long, but it's not too short, either. A 5K is just a little over three miles (3.1, to be exact), and it’s actually a great distance for runners of all ages, fitness levels, and experience levels, according to Erica Coviello, owner and running coach at Run Fit Stoked.

“I love a 5K because typically they're smaller races, so they really foster a sense of community, not just for runners, but anybody in the community,” she says. They’re also held pretty frequently, so if something goes wrong with training, the stakes aren’t as high as missing a once-a-year marathon.

Whether your goal is to run the full distance without stopping, achieve a certain time, or jump in on your family’s turkey trot, the right training plan will set you up for success. A 5K is an aerobic challenge, says Coviello. “That means you want to be able to get your heart in the condition to pump oxygen efficiently,” she says. By balancing different types of runs with cross-training, strength training, and rest days, a plan can make sure you’re working—but not overworking—your muscles while getting your heart into the right condition.

A good 5K training plan incorporates the right mix of workouts and (thankfully) plenty of rest days too. Read on for your running coach-approved training schedule and some tips to crush your next 5K.

Meet the experts: Erica Coviello, CPT, is a level 2 RRCA running coach and owner of Run Fit Stoked. Claudette Sariya, CPT, is a personal trainer, fitness coach, and organizer of Asian Women Stay Running (AWSTARS) Run Club in NYC.

In Coviello’s 5K training plan, you’ll typically be running four days a week and resting for three. Take a look at the chart for all the details, and keep scrolling for a breakdown of all the kinds of runs you’ll be doing.

Easy runs should be what they sound like: easy. They should be getting you to about 60 percent of your max heart rate, though you don’t need to focus on getting to an exact number, says Coviello. It’s more about the feel.

“Easy is when you're not huffing and puffing and it's not difficult to take a breath,” says Coviello. “Even if you're running alone, you should be able to have a full conversation with a person running next to you without having to gasp for breath every couple of words.”

This is what runners call a conversational pace. And, by the way, a majority of your runs in this training plan are easy.

Your long runs—done just once a week—should also be at a conversational pace, and they’re actually the most important run of all because they really train your muscles, connective tissue, and everything else you need to keep up with what you’re training your heart to do, says Coviello.

If a long run seems daunting to you, Coviello has two helpful tips. The first is that it is totally okay to walk if you need to take breaks. You’re still moving forward, after all. If you find that you need to walk a lot, that’s also potentially a sign that you’re starting out too fast. The second tip: Do your long run with a friend. Bringing someone else along not only keeps you entertained but is also a good way to tell if you’re running at a conversational pace, because, well, you should be able to have a conversation with them.

Depending on the pace that’s comfortable for you, you might even find that you run farther than a 5K during your long runs. That’s a good thing, says Coviello. It will make sure your endurance is there on race day.

These days are less important for beginners (and can actually just be treated as easy days if you don’t have a time goal), but are the key for anyone who wants to run a 5K at a certain pace.

On each weekly speed run, you’ll rotate between running at a faster pace and a slower one. By increasing your speed for shorter amounts of time, you get your body and mind used to going at your goal race pace until you can do it for the entire time. “It just adds on a little bit each time so that by the time you get to that 5K, you're ready to run the whole race at that pace,” says Coviello.

Since the speed workout format may be new for some, here's an example of what it could look like:

You can take all three days on this plan as true rest days, but there’s also the option to do a form of cross-training too. Cross-training should be another form of aerobic exercise. Think swimming, biking, using the elliptical—any form of low-impact cardio that you enjoy works. And, remember to take one full day for solely rest. Your body needs it!

Strength training should be done two to three times a week, and only needs to take about 20 minutes, says Coviello. You can do strength training after a run or at least two hours before a run. You're going to want to focus on the most important muscles for running when you're strength training.

They include your legs, glutes, calves, and core, says Claudette Sariya, CPT, a personal trainer, fitness coach, and organizer of the Asian Women Stay Running Run Club in NYC. You can keep it simple and do bodyweight exercises, but adding resistance is the only way to grow your muscles, she adds.

The answer to this question is going to be different for everybody, says Coviello. Generally, if you shoot for a 30-minute 5K, that means you’re running slightly faster than a 10-minute mile, which is a great goal for newer runners. But, the beauty of this distance is that it can be done much faster or slower than at a ten-minute pace. “Walkers can do this too,” Coviello says.

Coviello’s plan is 8 weeks long, but you can likely speed up or slow down this training plan depending on your experience level. For example, if you’re already running a 5K distance but looking to shave off some time, you won’t need eight weeks to see results. If you’re a true beginner, you might want to give yourself more time—like 12 to 16 weeks instead—to really get your body used to the idea of moving quickly, Coviello says.

Her eight-week plan is a great starting point for those who already have a little bit of an aerobic base (a.k.a. you already do some running, walking, swimming, or other kinds of fitness activities).

Aside from the musts like a good pair of running sneakers, a lot of the running gear you’ll need is weather-dependent. In warmer weather, Coviello recommends light, moisture-wicking clothes. Plus, you’ll want to stay protected from the sun by wearing a hat or sunglasses and sunscreen. In colder weather, warm socks (like Smartwool), something to keep your ears and head warm, and lots of layers will be important.

In order to follow Coviello’s plan, you’ll also want some sort of timing and tracking device.

The best way to prevent injury is by getting a good warm-up in, and it only takes about 5 to 10 minutes. Some dynamic stretching and a slow jog or walk will help wake up your muscles before a run.

One thing you should skip in your warm-up? Static stretching, says Coviello. This might actually make you more prone to getting an injury and is best done after a run.

Additionally, at any point in your training, you shouldn’t push through pain. If something hurts, you should take a rest (which is often what your body needs to remedy the issue, says Coviello), but if pain continues, you should consult a physical therapist or doctor.

Olivia Luppino is an associate editor at Women’s Health. She reports, writes, and edits content ranging from service stories to in-depth features and awards packages across all verticals, including health, fitness, sports, and beauty. While at WH, she’s traveled to Paris, France to watch Faith Kipyegon attempt a sub-4-minute mile and covered major races like Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc in Chamonix. Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. Her works also appears in New York Magazine’s The Cut, PS (formerly Popsugar), and Salon, where she also did on-camera interviews with celebrity guests.

#4928. 15 Best Floor Cushions for Grounded Seating (2026)

If it’s a lack of seating that inhibits you from hosting game night or book club, a collection of floor cushions is all you need to create ample options for guests. Historically linked to meditation and traditional Japanese dining culture, today’s iterations are all about modern living needs that suit every whim—lounging while binging on the Love Island reruns, creating a reading nook, or simply losing oneself in texts at the end of the day. Whether you’re looking for a versatile indoor/outdoor design that can withstand the elements or a kid-friendly sham that can easily blend in with your grown-up furniture and decor, we found 15 attractive options for top-tier floor pillows—many priced under $100. Ready for extra seating that feels a little more grounded? Keep scrolling for our recommendations.

Your space doesn’t have to follow Japandi or Wabi Sabi principles to benefit from these Shibori patterned floor cushions. The bold print draws attention without being overly loud and the square shape is optimal for distributing weight—even if you fidget. If you’re looking for more of a classic shape to double as couch pillows, the print is also available in an oversized floor pillow as well.

We’re picturing these circular stacking cushions scattered around the living room as movie night seating, surrounding a coffee table for casual mini meals, or serving as an island for your toddler’s playtime. Stitched-on handles make it easy to transport each pillow from room to room, and the two neutral textile options can easily coordinate with any sofa upholstery.

It might look like you stole these stylish tufted cushions from an exclusive beachfront resort on the Portuguese Coast, but we’ll know that your prowess lies in knowing how to shop Lulu & Georgia’s outdoor collection like a pro. Picture pool days sprawled out on your outdoor sofa with one of these demure pillows propping up your latest read. When the sun sets, guests will tote their cushions inside for a friendly card game around the coffee table.

Whether you’re outfitting a camping tent or looking to build out seating options inside the home, these foldable cushions won’t take up a ton of space. When they’re not being used during hot pot and trivia nights, they’re slim enough to slip in a hiking sack or tuck under the couch.

These square floor cushions bring a touch of traditional Japanese craftsmanship to your home decor. We like that they’re available in a midcentury-esque color palette that mixes new with old and that the natural cotton cover and fill are eco-friendly and easy to clean.

Woven from a rush straw cattail blend, these two round floor cushions will fulfill any, and all, desires to incorporate more natural materials into your design. They pair particularly well with a jute rug or a woven pendant crafted in a similar fashion. Plus, they’re a nice addition to your outdoor furniture setup under a covered balcony or patio.

Greenrow’s triangular floor pouf supports upright sitting in the way a traditional chair might, though its laid-back silhouette evokes a more casually elegant vibe. Stuffed with polystyrene beads, you could easily interpret the design as your beloved childhood beanbag, all grown up with a woven wool cover that fits well in bohemian-style spaces.

Cabana’s pouf, designed in collaboration with Ralph Lauren, includes three cushions stacked atop one another. Inspired by coastal design, the washed plaid upholstery is wedged between two needlepoint Edgecombe fabric cushions, manifesting slow summer days at the Cape with the whole extended family.

If you’re working with minimal square footage and hoping to maximize floor seating we like these neutral striped cushions that look like miniature poufs. They’re from C&B’s kids section, so you can rest assured that they’re durable enough to take on increased wear and tear. Plus they come in customizable upholstery, if checkered prints are more your thing.

Here’s another vibrant accent cushion that can add thoughtful color to neutral palette homes or support maximalist interior styles. The gusseted silhouette brings structure to the design, while the rustic textile speaks to bohemian design.

Good design moments are inevitable when a fashion designer and architect team up. Case in point: We have fashion designer Laura Juslin and architect Lilli Maunula to thank for this stylish pouf, which celebrates expert color combinations like mossy green and sky blue as well as dark plum and mint green. Exposed seam quilting proves to be a surprisingly fashionable choice wherever you intend to designate cozy seating, whether it’s your living room, bedroom, or even a spot for your furry friend to relax.

Some throw pillow shapes—namely ones that can do double duty as floor cushions when the occasion rises—are just superior, in our opinion. These lofty flax linen circular pillows add some cozy appeal to whatever loveseat or armchair they’re adorning, but they’ll also be a real flex when you’re hosting a larger group of guests and need more seat options.

This colorblock cushion may have been designed with meditation in mind, but it’s attractive enough to employ outside of zen practices. The dual-patterned colorblock prints bring a bohemian spirit that will liven up less colorful spaces or tease out the maximalist vibes in your space.

Made with textiles sourced from Japan, the Kiriko pleated folds on this meditation cushion bring a charming, floppy touch to the design while the side loop ensures it’s easy to tote from room to room. We can picture it making the seat of your office chair more comfortable or curling up next to the fireplace with a warming cup of tea and a good book.

Inspired by the colorful textiles characterizing the ’60s and ’70s, these tufted floor pillows can transform your living room into a groovy conversation pit. Each pattern is hand-painted, and the fluffy wool construction is a great way to incorporate texture into your living space.

#4927. I Play Rocky Tells Sylvester Stallone’s Underdog Story. Here’s What Really Happened.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The new biopic revisits the actor-turned-screenwriter’s long-shot fight to get Rocky made.

ROCKY IS A work of fiction, but it’s fair to say Sylvester Stallone threw his heart and suffering onto the page. The then-29-year-old actor-turned-screenwriter was a Hollywood nobody when the boxing movie to end all boxing movies was filmed in early 1976 (with a release later that year). The now-80-year-old had done minor work in largely forgotten films (oh, and don’t forget the porno!) when he wrote himself his dream leading role and convinced studio United Artists to both make the film and take a chance on the hardscrabble, sometimes literally hard-to-understand Sly as the star.

This story is recounted in I Play Rocky, the acclaimed biopic about the making and myth behind both Rocky and Stallone, played by Anthony Ippolito. Sly is, alas, “all but indivisible” from his creation, as The Guardian wrote in a review of his new memoir The Steps, “each an extension of the other.” Sly has his own version of his ascension, though it’s laced with frustration toward family and a certain producer on Rocky who he feels fleeced him (more on that below).

But what’s real in I Play Rocky, what’s Hollywood-ified, and what’s Stallone’s own hard-won truth? Here we dig into the facts behind Rocky’s development, production, and eventual gangbusters box office reception. But we also get into Stallone’s life and career, where the two merge with Rocky “The Italian Stallion” Balboa, and how well I Play Rocky streamlines it all into popcorn fun.

Sylvester Stallone was born on July 6, 1946, in Hell’s Kitchen, then a rough neighborhood of Manhattan, according to History. As Sly tells it in a (fascinating) 1976 profile in The New York Times, he was raised by a “bickering Italian couple” who put him in foster care while they worked. (His hairdresser father, Francesco “Frank” Stallone Sr., who had a tiny part in Rocky as the timekeeper in the climax fight, is embodied by a curmudgeonly Matt Dillon in I Play Rocky. Stallone’s mother, Jacqueline “Jackie” Stallone, is not depicted, but had a wild career as an astrologer, trapeze artist, chorus girl, women’s-only gym owner, and boxing promoter.)

Stallone became a juvenile delinquent who “attended 12 schools by the time he was 15, and was kicked out of most of them.” More promisingly, the native New Yorker went to high school in Philadelphia, aka Rocky town, and threw himself into sports, being a star fullback and discus thrower. In an unexpected move, he then attended university at American College in Leysin, Switzerland, where he picked up a part-time job chasing men away from female dorms, except he found it was more lucrative to “look the other way” for two francs an hour (money that scored him flights back home).

Studying drama came next at the University of Miami for two years before he moved back to New York with the loftiest thespian ambitions. Instead, he found work as an usher at a movie theater and cleaning cages at a zoo. I Play Rocky starts with a very broke Stallone as a theater usher, though there’s no zoo action, and the story cuts the very funny fact that he was fired from said theater when he tried scalping a M*A*S*H ticket to the theater’s owner, Walter Reade. (Also absent from I Play Rocky: Among Sly’s ambitions was a fully autobiographical film that he planned to call From Roaches to Riches.)

Sly took what scraps he could in his New York days, appearing in the low-budget greaser comedy The Lords of Flatbush (contrary to I Play Rocky, he was a co-lead actor, and even wrote additional dialogue). He had an uncredited bit part in Robert Altman’s 1970 touchstone M*A*S*H (yes, the same film for which he tried to scalp a ticket) and acted in Capone (1975) as well as the Roger Corman-produced cult B-movie Death Race 2000 (1975), playing Machine Gun Joe Viterbo.

That’s not all. In desperate need of money, the real-life Italian Stallion took a part in the 1970 softcore porn film The Party at Kitty and Stud's, in which he lets it all hang out—without hardcore sex. He thought the movie was garbage, but he was paid $200 for two days of work. “It was either do that movie or rob someone because I was at the end—at the very end—of my rope,” Stallone told Playboy in 1978, according to Far Out.

California came calling. With the tiniest bit of fame to his name, he moved to Los Angeles with his first wife Sasha Czack (AnnaSophia Robb in I Play Rocky), where they lived in a one-and-a-half-bedroom apartment ''in the pancreas of Hollywood,” he told the Times, for $215 a month.

“Things weren’t going so well there,” Stallone said in an archival interview quoted by Forbes. “As a matter of fact, I actually had to go out and try to sell my dog because it was either that or he wasn’t going to be very well fed around the house.” In I Play Rocky, you see Stallone actually sell his bull mastiff Butkus—then locate and buy back Butkus, who would go on to be the dog in Rocky—though it’s hard to verify if things unfolded exactly this way.

Inspiration came when he watched the championship fight in 1975 between Chuck Wepner, a club fighter known as ''The Bayonne Bleeder,'' and world champion Muhammad Ali. “And for one brief moment, this supposed stumblebum turned out to be magnificent,” Stallone later mused, according to Forbes. “I thought if this isn’t a metaphor for life.”

The lore is that based on this brief moment, Stallone went on a tear, writing the script for Rocky in just three and a half days. This becomes a crucial montage in I Play Rocky, with Sly putting pen to paper, then snoozing while his wife types it out. In reality, Stallone told the Times that he had the fight in his mind for a month, “my inspiration stage. Then I let it incubate for 10 months, the incubation stage.” Finally, writing the screenplay in a mad dash was merely “the verification stage.” Stallone loves stages.

Much of the tension in I Play Rocky actually takes place before a frame of Rocky is shot. Stallone gets a meeting with two producers, Irwin Winkler (P.J. Byrne) and Robert Chartoff (Toby Kebbell). When he turns out not to be right for a part, on his way out of the office, he sneaks his Rocky script onto the secretary’s desk. In reality, Stallone had already sold several scripts, mostly to television, and he simply brought up the Rocky idea to the producers, who asked to see the script.

Winkler and Chartoff, playing up much of the comedic antics and sarcasm in I Play Rocky, loved Stallone’s screenplay. They just didn’t want him to play the title role. The producers were contracted with United Artists. The top brass threw out star names like Burt Reynolds, James Caan, and Ryan O’Neal. But Sly refused to sell the script unless he took the lead role. And refused again. Despite the fact that he had no car and $106 in the bank.

In I Play Rocky, the figure offered to buy the script on the condition that Stallone will not play Rocky goes up to an astronomical $360,000 (which in today’s money would be $2.1 million); however, the 1976 Times profile reports that bidding went up to $265,000. Either way, a beautiful heap of money. Finally, the producers and studio greenlit Rocky with a $1 million budget and paid Stallone $35,000 plus his acting fee. Stallone was Rocky Balboa. The entire production lasted 28 days, ''the gestation time for a water bug,'' Sly quipped to the Times.

Here’s the more important part of the contract that’s left out of I Play Rocky: Stallone sold the rights to Rocky and its characters in accepting the deal. Those rights went to Winkler—which has given Winkler a fortune plus power over the Rocky franchise, and fueled an ongoing feud between the 95-year-old producer and Stallone, who’s not shy about badmouthing Winkler. Stallone is adamant that he was swindled. Per Collider, he has called Winkler “nefarious” and “the remarkably untalented and parasitical producer of Rocky and Creed.”

Despite this supposed crooked deal, Stallone came out more than fine. What’s also not in I Play Rocky is that the then-unknown received 10 percent of the net profits (known as net points in the industry) of Rocky off the backend. Given that Rocky earned roughly $200 million at the box office, becoming the top hit of 1976—on top of picking up 10 Oscar nominations and an illustrious run in ancillary business—Stallone easily made millions off the original movie. (He claims $2.5 million, Winkler claims much higher.) He is leaps and leaps richer now.

Many forget that upon release, the critical response to Rocky was mixed, split between pans and raves. But it energized the masses, and its classic status is unquestionable now. In real life and I Play Rocky, Stallone comes out with a smile on his face, aware that his and his character’s rise from forgotten bum to underdog sensation is complete.

Are you a fan of Rocky? (If you’re not, how the hell are you reading this article?) How about Rocky II, Rocky III, the Creed movies? If so, yes, yes, yes, and again yes. On the other hand, if you don’t like Hollywood behind-the-scenes tales, boxing, or Rocky, sit this one out.

Though it fudges some turns of events and details, smoothing out the messiness and politics of a young star making his way in Hollywood, its insights can be revelatory—for example, the incredible contribution of Steadicam inventor Garrett Brown (played by real-life camera operator Michael Fuchs), whose experimental invention not only made the original Rocky possible on its shoestring budget and tight schedule, but brought a distinctive graceful movement to Rocky’s training and fighting scenes. The cast is all-around captivating; Ippolito finds soul in Stallone while managing a difficult and eccentric accent.

Perhaps unsurprisingly, as directed by Peter Farrelly (Green Book), I Play Rocky is also on the schmaltzy side. But when Sly, or Ippolito, sprints up those Philadelphia Art Museum steps determined to change history, your heart will do a little jump for him.

Paul Schrodt is a freelance writer and editor covering pop culture and the entertainment industry. He has contributed to The New York Times, The Wall Street Journal, GQ, Men's Health, The Hollywood Reporter, Los Angeles magazine, and others.
